Owner ruling on discovering this: the item must be flagged as failed and documented with the failure reason and the rules to prevent recurrence — surfacing that CLOSED is itself a hard-terminal ledger state with no correction path, so the only fix that matters is closing the gap before an item ever reaches CLOSED. Fix: a pure `staleRecoveryGates` function — the developer gate is ALWAYS required fresh; the architect gate is required fresh only when the item's own original gates map already carried that key. A second deterministic fold-time override sits beside the existing verify-override: for any recovery-shaped result with a real code change, it diffs the worktree, takes the latest changed-file mtime, stats the required gate files, and rejects a carried-forward or absent verdict file before the fold ever reaches CLOSED, regardless of how green the machine evidence is. The generated recovery protocol now explicitly warns of this in both branches. This closes the gap for fold-time detection going forward; it did not retroactively correct the two already-mis-closed items — that correction shipped as a companion fix (manual reopen capability) the same session: both items were reopened, the divergence fixed at the root, re-gated by independent fresh agents, and folded genuinely CLOSED. |
| KI-E462 | **Ported from a host-mount session — the prior fix (KI-E461) is pure mitigation: it fails the fold AFTER a full gate cycle has already run. Owner directive on reviewing it: prevention is much cheaper than mitigation — closing the barn door at fold time still burns the gate-agent tokens on a recovery that was always going to be rejected.** This is the prevention half: a cheap, deterministic, git-diff-only scan (no LLM) runnable in seconds immediately after a remedy is written, before a single gate-agent token is spent. A new lint extracts every service-registration call from a modified startup/registration file, normalizes each argument list's shape (literals collapsed to type markers, so two calls passing their own distinct names are correctly seen as the same shape), and flags a call whose method is also invoked elsewhere in the repo but whose shape matches none of those sibling sites — the exact signature of an undocumented divergence. Configuration-lambda bodies are excluded entirely, since a lambda is expected to vary per call site and comparing lambda bodies structurally produced near-total noise in testing — live-verified this dropped one incident's raw hit count from 7 to 1 and another's from 9 to 1, with the one surviving hit in both cases being the real incident. Deliberately advisory, never a hard block. Wired as a build-test lint and into the recovery protocol strictly between "apply the remedy" and the expensive re-gate step — the actual cost-shift the owner asked for. A false-negative was caught before shipping: the first version silently reported a clean scan on both real incident worktrees because an unhandled buffer-size error on a large file listing was swallowed by the tool's own best-effort error handling. Fixed with an explicit larger buffer and a distinct internal-error marker so a scan failure is never again indistinguishable from a genuine clean result. |
| KI-E463 | **Ported from a host-mount session — a manual "reopen" escape hatch for a wrongly-CLOSED item.** CLOSED has zero outbound transitions by design — correct for the automatic fold path, but it meant a wrongly-closed item (see the companion entry on carried-forward gates) had no correction path short of a direct ledger edit, which is exactly what "the driver is the single ledger writer" forbids. The reopen command is the narrow, human-invoked exception: requires both an explicit reason (mandatory audit trail) and an explicit force flag (deliberate-action confirmation) — refuses without either; accepts CLOSED or READY as the from-state only (never an in-progress state — this is a terminal/just-reopened-only correction, not a generic force-transition tool); resets the attempt counter and gates map (no stale verdict lingers to be misread as current); and appends an unambiguous reopened-manually history note with the reason. Defaults to READY but accepts other target states directly, so a reopened item that already carries a verified in-worktree remedy can go straight to the direct-recovery path instead of a wasted full pass. |
| KI-E464 | **Ported from a host-mount session — a documented "parallel Workflow instances are fine" guidance line was never reconciled with a later owner directive making "one workflow at a time" the actual enforced default**, with the newer guidance already stated as a hard rule in the controller's installed skill manual but NOT in the higher-level run-guide a session is told to read first. A session reading only the run-guide would reasonably conclude parallel Workflows are the sanctioned default throughput lever, when the actually-current guidance is the opposite: concurrency lives inside one dispatch's own lane-count knob, not across simultaneous Workflow launches. Found live while orienting for a large mechanical-backlog run, before launching anything on the stale guidance. Fix: the run-guide now states the current default explicitly, cross-references the installed skill manual, and reframes the "parallel instances" mechanism as a deliberate, documented exception (still mechanically available for genuinely disjoint target sets on different schedules) rather than routine throughput tuning. Doc-only change. |
| KI-E465 | **Ported from a host-mount session — the recovery-gate-freshness check (mtime-only, from the companion entry above) false-positived twice in one session on a byte-identical re-save, forcing an unnecessary full extra gate-developer dispatch each time.** Two items both recovered cleanly from a pure infra-outage death (gates already approved, only the final stage missing, zero code remedy needed) — but an earlier controller mistake the same session (a relaunch, stopped within a minute or two once the waste was noticed) had briefly let each item's fixer re-write its already-correct fix with byte-identical content before being killed, bumping the file's modification time past the gate verdict that had already reviewed that exact content. The freshness check correctly has no way to distinguish "same bytes, new timestamp" from "genuinely new, unreviewed code" from mtime alone, so it rejected both — each requiring a very expensive fresh gate dispatch to re-derive a verdict the first one had already given. Fix: a deterministic content hash over the changed-file set, plus two new optional parameters on the freshness check — fully backward compatible. The fold now reads a per-item sidecar (role -> content hash, written the last time that gate genuinely passed freshness) before rejecting on mtime alone; a match proves the gate already reviewed this exact content regardless of what the filesystem clock says, and a miss falls through to the original mtime verdict unchanged — purely additive, it can only rescue a false positive, never mask a genuine undisclosed change, since a real edit changes the content hash too. Every freshness pass re-stamps the current hash so a later recovery attempt on the same item benefits going forward. Disclosed gap: stamping currently only fires on the recovery path, not on a plain first-pass close, so an item's very first close never gets a stamp and the false-positive can still occur once before any stamp exists for that item. |
| KI-E466 | **Ported from a host-mount session — the scheduler's "fall back to a mixed batch when no cluster covers the priority" guidance had no actual tooling behind it, so the controller had to hand-roll a query against the live item graph — and that hand-rolled query is exactly where a tier-eligibility filter is easy to drop.** The scheduler's own readiness computation correctly excludes owner-gated and blocked-tier items (which sync to the same "ready" ledger state as auto-eligible items, distinguished only by a tier field) — but a query that checks only the ledger state re-includes them. Live: a session's hand-picked batch request had most of its items rejected at claim time, mostly because they were owner-gated (never auto-batchable) with the rest on unmet dependencies. No corruption resulted (the claim step cleanly warns and skips before any work starts) but it cost a wasted round-trip, and the same under-filtered recipe was sitting in a committed handoff doc where the next session would hit it again. Fix: the scheduler's suggestion command now always prints a ready-made fallback batch command using the SAME correctly-filtered readiness list the cluster logic already computes, severity-bucketed and capped at a sane default size — additive-only, read-only, the existing cluster suggestions are unchanged. |
| KI-E467 | **Ported from a host-mount session — a documented account of what happens when a vendored copy of this engine and its public upstream have diverged in BOTH directions and a routine sync must become a real merge rather than a fast-forward.** The general lesson: taking either side wholesale silently deletes the other side's independently-developed features; the correct sync takes one side as the base and re-applies every feature the other side built on top, then adds regression coverage for each so a future sync cannot silently drop them again (a dedicated "host-only surface" block of assertions is the backstop). Certain categories of local configuration and local-only files are deliberately never taken from upstream during a sync (local config overrides, local gitignore conventions, host-specific runtime state and planning artifacts) — only the engine mechanism itself is reconciled. |
| KI-E468 | **Ported from a host-mount session — routing the "mechanical" (small, bounded) task tier to the cheapest capable model is a re-attempt of a change that was tried once before and reverted.** The first attempt closed 1 of 17 items in its trial batch, with several failures carrying the fixer's own report claiming full completion while the deterministic gates disagreed — a narrative-vs-verdict mismatch. The revert noted the result was confounded by an unrelated variable (a different feature was ALSO being trialed in the same batch) and set an explicit precondition for ever re-attempting it: isolate that other variable first. The second attempt is only made once that precondition is met structurally (the host's own environment constraints make the confounding variable impossible to reintroduce), and two additional pre-band safety nets had been built in the meantime that specifically target the observed failure mode (a false "complete" diff is now caught cheaply before the expensive gate band runs, and a narrative/verdict contradiction detector runs at fold). The change is explicitly framed as evaluated against the two recorded prior outcomes, with an explicit revert condition if it regresses again. |
| KI-E469 | **Ported from a host-mount session — companion to the entry above: routing the mechanical tier's own EXECUTION to the cheapest model, without ALSO giving it a planning pass first, does not produce the intended "small model as hands, mid-tier model as the brain" split — it was accidentally skipping planning for that tier entirely since the tier's introduction.** The result would have been the smallest model authoring both the regression test and the fix with no stated approach in front of it, with the mid-tier model appearing only downstream as a reviewer/judge of the work, never its director. Fix: the mechanical tier now gets its own mid-tier planner (deliberately not the most expensive planning tier — a mechanical item is bounded by definition, so it needs a competent approach, not the top tier reserved for the hardest cases). A wanted second-order effect: having a real plan also switches on an existing pre-band check that verifies the delivered diff matches its own stated approach, which previously had nothing to check against for this tier. Caught while verifying this pair: an earlier change had only been applied to some of the places a model-routing table is mirrored (the main engine's routing table, but not an alternate-runtime port's own copy) — a live model fork between two runtimes that an existing parity gate did not catch, because that gate compared different things. A new three-way parity gate now pins every mechanical/non-mechanical routing tier across all the places it is mirrored simultaneously. |
| KI-E470 | **Ported from a host-mount session — a multi-step plan's steps now DRIVE execution directly, not just check it afterward.** An earlier change had built step-by-step plan decomposition but deliberately bound it to checking only ("steps decompose checking, never execution"), on the grounds that a worker holding only its own slice of a multi-step plan cannot run the fixer's whole-item cross-file guards that protect against fix-introduced defects — the single most common rejection class observed. A later owner review judged that the multi-step EXECUTION was the actual intent, and that reasoning is answered rather than overridden: no worker ever holds only its own slice (every step call receives the full plan, with each step marked done/current/pending plus the cumulative files touched so far — one author working in passes with the whole item permanently in view), and the whole-diff consistency sweep still runs as its own dedicated final pass with the complete diff in view and no competing authoring work. Why the extra calls are worth it: this pipeline's most expensive observed authoring failure is partial work reported as complete — a fix covering one of several named targets, a fix addressing one branch of parallel logic and missing its twin, a safety mechanism threaded at some call sites and not others. A per-step claim is falsifiable against ONE named change; a whole-item claim is not. Especially load-bearing once the smallest model is doing the hands-on execution for this tier. Bounded by a configurable step cap (zero restores the single-call behavior exactly); a reused prior fix still short-circuits the whole mechanism; per-step returns merge into one unified result so every downstream consumer of that result is unchanged; a mid-chain failure reports how far it got for the retry round; an unavailable step is treated as an infra failure, never a quality verdict. Also fixed while pinning this: a test-harness recording gap meant a harness could assert WHICH agent roles ran but nothing about what they were actually told — a branch dispatching the right role with the wrong instructions would have passed every behavioral check; the harness now records a bounded slice of the actual prompt text. |
| KI-E471 | **Ported from a host-mount session — a fixer's self-reported list of changed files was never deterministically compared against the real diff.** The only existing cross-check looked the OTHER direction (a real edit missing from the claimed list), never a claimed file with no edit behind it — so a fixer claiming three files changed while having touched only one passed every gate, probe, and fold. This is the same "partial work reported as complete" failure mode named in the companion entry above, stated at its most literal: the claim itself was simply false and nothing checked. Fix: a pure comparison function plus a new fold-time check, reusing a file-diff list the fold already computes elsewhere so the check costs nothing extra. Deliberately conservative in both directions (tolerant of path-prefix differences, separator differences, and relative-vs-absolute forms; anchored so a short filename never spuriously matches a longer one that merely contains it as a substring) since a false rejection costs a full re-band. Caught while wiring it: the claimed-files list was never actually being attached to the per-item result in the first place, which would have shipped the whole check as a silent no-op. |
| KI-E472 | **Ported from a host-mount session — the companion whole-diff consolidation pass from KI-E470 now explicitly runs on a stronger model tier than the granular per-step hands, never weaker.** Consolidation is the one call that must hold the entire change in view simultaneously — sibling-pattern sweep, dead-code trace, re-checking earlier claims, cross-step coherence — which is strictly harder than any individual step; leaving it on the same cheap tier as the steps would put the least capable model on the hardest pass, inverting the intended hands/brains split. A new dedicated route is added with an explicit no-downgrade rule: a harder item's steps already run on the top tier, so its consolidation stays on the top tier too — the new route is a floor for the cheap tier, never a ceiling on the expensive one. The call site degrades gracefully to old behavior if a routing table predates the new route, rather than failing. |
| KI-E473 | **Ported from a host-mount session — the per-step execution calls (not the planning or consolidation passes) for the NON-mechanical tier get their own, deliberately mid-tier model** on the reasoning that a single bounded, individually-checkable step executed against a plan that already exists does not need the most expensive authoring tier. The two passes that must hold the whole item — planning and consolidation — are deliberately left on their existing top tiers. The blast radius of the change is narrowly scoped to the step-execution call site only; the non-stepwise single-call path and all bounded "amend" calls (which each re-read the complete diff and so are whole-diff work, not step work) explicitly keep the top tier, with a dedicated regression check that fails if a step-tier route ever leaks into one of those call sites. |
| KI-E474 | **Ported from a host-mount session — the documented recovery procedure for a killed/interrupted run was itself the path that threw the work away, rather than continuing it**, after an owner directive to fix exactly that. Two compounding defects: (1) a prior-attempt-artifact-reuse mechanism existed but was wired into only one recovery path, while the actually-documented recovery procedure used a different path that never populated it, so following the documented procedure guaranteed starting cold; (2) reuse was fenced on an artifact being newer than the item's last claim transition, but the standard recovery procedure stamps a FRESH claim as part of itself — making every on-disk artifact from the killed run look stale by construction even though nothing had touched the worktree and the real work was still sitting in it. Live cost measured at the time: over a dozen items re-queued while still carrying substantial real, unreused diffs. Fix: the staleness fence is no longer a clock but the worktree's own content — an artifact is reusable exactly when every file it claims is still present in the live diff, evaluated independently per artifact type (a fix can be proven reusable while a test is not). Strictly stronger than a timestamp, immune to claim-timing churn, and it cannot resurrect genuinely vanished work because the files being absent from the diff IS the disproof. The verification/review stages downstream are unchanged by design — a reused prior-stage artifact is still independently re-verified and re-reviewed from scratch; only the expensive authoring stages are skipped when their output is still provably present. |
| KI-E475 | **Ported from a host-mount session — three defects in the prior-attempt-reuse family above, all found on its FIRST live exercise, two of them latent false-rejections that would have failed a genuinely good recovery.** (1) A claimed-files field is typed as a plain string list but the authoring role is also asked to annotate each entry with a short rationale, so a real claim naturally reads as "path (note): explanation" rather than a bare path — the new phantom-claim check (from a companion entry) treated the whole annotated string as a literal path, so a correctly-changed file could never match the diff and would be wrongly reported as a phantom claim, a false rejection costing a full re-band produced by the very check meant to catch dishonesty. Fixed by parsing just the leading path-shaped token before any trailing annotation; the check itself is unweakened — a genuinely absent file is still correctly flagged. (2) The reuse-eligibility scan ran AFTER the current claim step itself, so the very newest claim transition was always milliseconds old and newer than every real artifact by construction — a dry-run preview said most items were reusable while the real run moments later said far fewer were, because the preview and the run were measuring against two different claim timestamps. Fixed by fencing against the claim timestamp from strictly BEFORE the current command started. (3) Reused prior-stage prose, inlined per item at full length, pushed a large batch's generated launch script over the dispatch channel's hard size cap — a reuse mechanism that makes the batch impossible to launch at all is strictly worse than no reuse. Fixed with an explicit cap on how much prior-stage prose is inlined per item, mirroring an existing cap used elsewhere for the same reason; the margin against the hard cap is left narrow and disclosed, with no graceful degradation yet if it grows further. |
| KI-E476 | **Ported from a host-mount session — the companion fix above gated reuse of the PLANNING artifact on the TEST artifact specifically being reusable, which caused unnecessary re-planning on items that had a complete, perfectly good plan sitting on disk.** This coupling was wrong on two counts: requiring the test to be reusable conflated "did the planning stage complete?" (which a later-stage artifact of ANY kind existing already proves, since a later stage cannot run before an earlier one) with "is the test still valid?" — two unrelated facts; and a plan is an artifact of reasoning, not of file edits, so asking the same worktree-diff proof used for edits to validate a plan is a category error — there are no files for a plan to be checked against. Live cost: several items each held a complete plan plus two further completed stages and still re-ran the (expensive) planning step, because their specific test artifact's own file reference happened to be an item-directory path that could never appear in a worktree diff. Fix: the reuse gate widens to accept either a reusable test OR a reusable fix as sufficient proof that planning completed — a later-stage artifact is itself proof, by construction, that every earlier stage already ran. Safety is unchanged: an item whose worktree holds no diff at all skips reuse entirely regardless, so a plan left over from a genuinely stale, long-since-reset attempt is still unreachable. |
| KI-E477 | **Ported from a host-mount session — Docker vanishing MID-BAND (present at RED/baseline-capture time with a genuinely green 0-failure baseline, but gone by integrate time) produced a false regression the existing baseline-tolerance mechanism could not explain, because that mechanism only corrects a baseline captured WHILE Docker was already absent at RED time; it had no mechanism for an environment that flips AFTER a legitimate baseline was captured.** Live incident: an item with every gate APPROVED, refute OK, re-audit converged, was deterministically overridden to FAILED on "hundreds of new suite failures beyond baseline 0". Independently verified three ways: a fresh re-run on the same Docker-less host reproduced the identical failure count; every failure, paired against the test runner's own per-failure error anchor, carried a Docker/Testcontainers-unavailable exception signature; the item's own designated regression-test classes independently showed 0 failures both originally and on re-run. A second, compounding gap surfaced mid-fix: the suite-runner script piped full test output through a bounded grep/tail before writing the transcript to disk, so the actual on-disk artifact retained only bare error-header lines with the exception text already stripped — a classifier reading the transcript has no evidence to work from regardless of pattern-matching quality. Two-part fix: (a) a new pure helper pairs each error anchor against a nearby Docker-unavailable signature and the verdict function gains an optional exclude-count parameter, GATED on an independent live Docker-availability probe taken at fold time (never the transcript's own claim), so a diff that genuinely broke container wiring while Docker is UP still fails normally; (b) the suite-runner script now computes the Docker-environmental count from the FULL untruncated output BEFORE the display grep/tail trims it, via a single-source-of-truth CLI wrapper, emitting it as an optional trailing field on the existing keyed summary marker — fully backward-compatible (a transcript with no such field parses identically to before). |
| KI-E478 | **Ported from a host-mount session — the gate band was the FIRST stage to ever see amended code; the build+test runner ran before the pre-band amend chain and nothing re-built afterward.** The pre-band probes that dispatch a fixer to edit the worktree (edge-scan, acceptance-gap, plan-commitment) all ran AFTER the only build+test stage in the pipeline — so when an amend broke the build, the expensive multi-reviewer gate panel was the first thing to find out, paying for the whole band to discover it. Root-caused from three failures in one batch, each judged "the fix is correct and meets every acceptance clause" while actually broken by a LATER amend: one item's new regression test failed 100% at runtime; another's worktree had drifted to a broken draft (a script that could not execute, a test file failing to build); a third had a duplicate, self-contradictory section that postdated every prior artifact. Fix: a post-amend re-verify — one cheap-tier runner call, gated on the diff having actually changed AND at least one amend having fired, teeing to its own separate transcript (never appended into the pre-amend baseline transcript the fold parses) — failing the item PRE-BAND with the build/test verdict when the amend chain left the tree broken. Zero added cost on the common path: it fires only when an amend actually edited, so an item whose amends never ran behaves identically to before. |
| KI-E479 | **Ported from a host-mount session — a documentation-synchronization contract (every change to a controller/handler/consumer/event/job/HTTP client must update its corresponding doc file) was enforced only at the gate band, at full expensive-reviewer price.** The review gates already treat a stale doc as blocking, but nothing checked it before the band. Measured cost: an item judged "excellent, correct, independently-verified rewrite" with every targeted test green failed anyway — "only because the documentation still described the retired mechanism." A full multi-reviewer gate panel plus adversarial review plus adjudication, spent to discover a missing doc edit a path comparison finds for free. Fix: a pure deterministic-scan module plus a CLI lint plus a pre-band cheap-tier classify stage, the same deterministic-scan/LLM-classify split already used for registration-drift and ledger-anchor checks. The lint says which service changed a documented surface without touching its doc; the classifier decides whether the change actually altered documented behavior, so a pure refactor or rename is not a finding. Deliberately conservative: only enumerated trigger path-shapes count, test files are excluded, and a service whose doc file doesn't exist can never be flagged (absence of a doc is not itself a violation). Validated against the real incident, not a fixture: replaying the actual changed-file list with the doc removed yields exactly one gap; with the doc present, zero. |
| KI-E480 | **Ported from a host-mount session — an oversize launcher script left the operator holding a claimed, unlaunchable batch.** The dispatch channel caps script size, and the launcher inlines the engine script PLUS the whole batch's reused prior-attempt payload — so batch size and prior-work reuse compete for one shared budget, and nothing checked it. Exceeding the cap surfaced as an opaque host-side rejection AFTER every item had already been claimed and had its worktree built. Live: a batch with reuse emitted a script within a few percent of the cap, and an earlier attempt exceeded it outright, blocking the run entirely. Fix: prior-attempt reuse payload is now shed largest-first until the emitted script fits, accounting for the base script's own size, announcing every drop. Reuse is the correct thing to shed first: losing it costs one item a re-derived plan/test/fix (expensive but the item still runs), whereas an oversize script costs the whole batch. When the batch is still too large with all reuse shed, the tool says so plainly rather than launching a script the host will refuse. |
| KI-E481 | **Ported from a host-mount session — a pre-band plan-commitment check was switched off for exactly the lane that needed it most: a "verification-only" lane where the fixer made no code changes because it attested the issue is already resolved in the standing tree.** The probe was gated to skip whenever there was no fix diff to check, reasoned as "no fixer, nothing to probe" — backwards for the actual risk: a verification-only attestation is an UNVERIFIED CLAIM about a diff that already exists, and routing it straight to the full expensive gate band to adjudicate that claim is the costliest possible way to catch a wrong one. Measured: three verification-only items each carried a fresh plan naming literally checkable work the standing diff did not contain; two burned a full expensive gate band before being caught. In the same batch this same probe caught an identical defect shape pre-band, cheaply, on a normal (non-verification-only) lane — the mechanism works; it was simply disabled for the lane where the risk is highest. Fix: gate on the plan existing alone, regardless of lane. On the verification-only lane, the prompt tells the probe to grade the plan's steps against the STANDING diff rather than against new work, treating a step whose prescribed change is absent as unevidenced rather than crediting an intention. |
| KI-E482 | **Ported from a host-mount session — the pipeline's most load-bearing artifact (the RED proof confirming a test genuinely fails before the fix) was agent-typed free text, and its machine-read verdict could be inverted by ordinary prose appended after it.** A parser used a last-match search over a bare, plain-text completion marker — text an agent can type anywhere — so an author appending a narrative description after the real transcript (ending in something that happened to echo the marker's own exit-code-zero form) silently inverted a genuine failing-test proof into "vacuous test," failing the item and feeding its retry loop the exact opposite of the truth. This is precisely the kind of marker-shadowing a KEYED completion-marker convention already existed elsewhere in the pipeline to prevent — only the build/test runner script emitted the keyed form; the RED-capture path simply never adopted its own convention. Fix: the keyed line now wins when present, with the bare marker retained as a fallback so older transcripts still parse. A second, compounding gap: the capture script never itself wrote the proof file — the proof only existed if the dispatched agent remembered to pipe output to the exact right path by hand, and several agents in one batch dropped or bungled this step, with the resulting MISSING file then misread as "vacuous" rather than "never captured." Fix: the capture command now self-tees to an optional path argument and announces that it did, so absence of evidence is distinguishable from a silent write failure. |
| KI-E483 | **Ported from a host-mount session — a read-only integrity probe was manufacturing the very evidence it was meant to audit.** Instructed to "run exactly this one command and do nothing else," a cheap-tier probe instead — on finding the expected proof transcript missing — re-ran the proof-capture command itself and wrote the output to the canonical path. That run executed against the ALREADY-FIXED tree, naturally produced a clean exit, and converted a truthful "evidence missing" into a false "the test is vacuous." Happened on three separate items in one batch, each leaving a botched first-attempt artifact on disk as the fingerprint of the probe's own improvised re-run. An integrity probe that can write the evidence it grades is not a check. Fix: the prompt now opens by naming the probe strictly read-only and forbidding it from creating, modifying, re-running, or regenerating ANY evidence, and states plainly that a missing marker IS the answer — with the reason spelled out (a regenerated proof runs against the fixed tree and inverts the verdict). The durable fix (removing the improvisation surface entirely via a deterministic, non-agent check) is named as the real target; the prompt hardening here is the interim mitigation. |
| KI-E484 | **Ported from a host-mount session — a model-tier routing experiment (routing a bounded, mechanical task class's test-authoring step to the cheapest capable model) was evaluated against its own pre-registered decision rule using real batch data, and partially reverted based on the result.** The experiment's own rule said: judge the next batch against both the old baseline and an even-cheaper baseline, and revert on regression. Applied to real numbers: against the cheapest baseline, the new routing IMPROVED; against the true prior baseline, it REGRESSED to roughly half, on a cohort that was if anything easier. The one statistically robust signal across the full sample indicted the test-authoring step specifically — a since-fixed class of "evidence-plumbing" failure occurred at a much higher rate under the cheaper model, with the same detection probe live in both eras making the comparison fair. A companion fixer-execution step's own readout was separately judged too confounded by concurrent, unrelated defects (found and fixed mid-window) to draw a conclusion either way, so it was deliberately left on the cheaper tier for one more tripwired batch rather than reverted on the same uncertain evidence — splitting what a prior change had deliberately bundled together specifically so the NEXT readout would be attributable to one variable at a time. A pre-registered tripwire: revert the surviving cheap-tier route if the next batch regresses again, or if a single specific false-completion-claim shape appears. |
| KI-E485 | **Ported from a host-mount session — a second, independent main-tree/worktree contamination incident, where an existing build-guard did not cover the actual vector and an existing post-amend re-verify safety net missed it too because it only fires when a specific flag was set by one of a FIXED list of amend call sites.** Live evidence, independently confirmed by the controller rather than trusted from any agent's self-report: the fold's own auto-repair mechanism fired mid-fold, restoring several real application files that some process had written directly to the MAIN tree during the run — not via a git reset/clean (a different, already-documented vector), but a direct file WRITE to the wrong absolute path, invisible to the existing build-guard because that guard only checks paths passed to the test-runner's own subcommands, never a raw file-edit tool call. Separately, two brand-new files were left sitting in the main tree with NO corresponding worktree copy at all — genuine new work created at the wrong absolute path from the very start, which the existing restore-to-HEAD repair (built for tracked-file modification, not untracked file creation) could not and did not clean up; the controller removed them by hand. A plausible, not proven, contributing factor: a documented "continue from existing worktree" reuse path keeps the SAME worktree path across attempts, so an incompletely-terminated PRIOR attempt's own process may still have been writing when a NEW attempt's launch began, racing the two. Fix (for the blast radius, not the unproven root cause): the post-amend re-verify now runs for EVERY code-change item unconditionally, dropping its prior "only if a specific amend call site set a flag" gate — it no longer needs to know WHY or by what mechanism the worktree might have drifted, only whether it currently builds and passes its targeted test, which is the fact that actually matters before the expensive gate band. The deeper gap — nothing mechanically prevents an agent's own file-edit tool call from landing at an absolute main-tree path instead of its assigned worktree path — is flagged as a real, unresolved, structural limitation outside what a prompt-level change can close. |
| KI-E486 | **Ported from a host-mount session — the last of a family of checks (the other two already hoisted pre-band in earlier fixes) that was still fold-time-only: a fixer's self-reported list of changed files, never independently verified against the real worktree diff before the entire expensive gate band ran.** The general failure class this guards against — partial work reported as complete — had repeatedly been the pipeline's most expensive observed defect, and this specific check was still only caught at the very end of the pipeline, after the full band, until now. Fix: a new deterministic lint (mirroring an existing sibling check's exact shape) plus a new pre-band cheap-tier probe dispatched right after an earlier root-cause-touch probe, checking the claimed-files list against the live diff before any expensive reviewer runs. A real design flaw was caught and fixed before shipping by checking against actual ground truth: the first draft read the claimed-files list from an on-disk artifact file that a multi-step fixer mode never actually writes (only per-step artifacts), which would have made the new probe silently inert for exactly the run shape — multi-step — the underlying defect class matters most for. Confirmed by checking whether a real incident's own multi-step worktree had ever produced the expected consolidated file (it had not). Fixed by having the probe write its own claimed-files scratch file via the one tool-write mechanism the host script DOES have, instead of reading a file that may never exist. The lint itself was validated directly against the real incident's worktree (correctly flagging the one real phantom file, correctly clearing a genuinely-changed one) before any automated test was written. |
| KI-E487 | **Ported from a host-mount session — a plan that decomposed its own work into exactly ONE bounded step did not qualify for a cheaper per-step execution route that existed for 2+-step plans, so it paid full top-tier-model whole-item-authoring price for work the planner had already scoped down to one clean unit.** Confirmed by reading the actual routing: a multi-step execution mechanism already routed a complex-tier plan's individual steps to a cheaper model than the top tier reserved for an undecomposed whole-item call — but the trigger required at least two steps, so a plan with exactly one well-defined step fell straight through to the expensive whole-item path, treated identically to a prose plan of completely unknown size. Fix: lowered the multi-step trigger from "two or more" to "one or more" — a plan with even a single usable step now runs through the existing per-step loop instead of the whole-item fallback. This surfaced a second, previously-moot question: what model should a mandatory post-step consolidation pass use when there was only one step to consolidate? The existing rule (never downgrade consolidation below the step tier) was reasoned for genuine multi-step drift across separate authoring passes — a single-step run has no second pass to have diverged FROM, so that specific hazard doesn't apply, though the sweep's other checks (dead-code, adjacent-claim, cross-reference) still have standalone value. A new, slightly cheaper consolidation route fills exactly this one-step gap; a plan with two or more steps is completely unaffected. |
| KI-E488 | **Ported from a host-mount session — three fixes to a multi-step fixer mechanism, all discovered by synthesizing why an entire batch closed zero of several items, after an explicit directive to find a way to improve the engine's quality.** Two independent diagnostic investigations, each reading the full artifact trail and the live worktree rather than trusting either item's own prose, converged on the same subsystem. (i) A false "files missing" halt that cost a full cycle: a step's own halt claimed a file "does not exist in the worktree," but the engine's own bookkeeping recorded a real content hash for that exact file many hours before the false claim. Root cause: the per-step prompt never carried the item's absolute worktree path, even though it was already in scope and used two calls later — a step whose completion depended on an earlier step's file had no anchor telling it where to look, and no instruction to treat a path-miss as suspect rather than conclusive. Fix: every per-step prompt now states the worktree root explicitly and warns that a path-miss is not proof of absence. (ii) The per-step and consolidation-pass artifact files never actually existed on disk for ANY item, because the underlying role brief's write instruction didn't distinguish between the three different dispatch shapes (whole-item / per-step / consolidation) that all share it — every step and the consolidation pass silently overwrote the SAME file, leaving only the LAST call's output on disk, confirmed via a built-in stale-artifact detector correctly flagging all expected files as missing on a live item. This crippled post-hoc forensics. Fix: each per-step call now carries an explicit override naming its own unique output filename. (iii) A step's NEGATIVE self-claim ("nothing changed," "the fix isn't applied") was trusted with zero cross-check — the mirror-image gap of an existing check that only ever cross-checked POSITIVE claims. A step halted a whole item insisting the worktree was clean and a specific fix wasn't present — both demonstrably false on inspection of the live diff. Fix: when a step self-reports non-application, the loop now reuses the existing phantom-claim probe against whatever files EARLIER steps already claimed; if the probe proves those files ARE in the diff, the step gets one bounded recheck with that objective evidence shown — never an automatic pardon, a recheck that still says no still fails the item. |
| KI-E489 | **Ported from a host-mount session — an existing "do not re-capture the baseline during a re-fix attempt" protection fired unconditionally for any retry, with no check that a baseline had EVER actually been captured on any round — so an item whose baseline was never captured had no path to ever get one, permanently.** Live incident: an item finally cleared every review gate cleanly for the first time across several cycles, and was then deterministically failed anyway on "new suite failures beyond baseline 0." Investigated to the root: no baseline file existed anywhere on disk for this item, on any round; the failures were genuine, real assertion failures — not an environmental/infrastructure signature; running the identical test class directly against a clean, unmodified copy of the main branch reproduced most of the same failures with the identical signature, definitively proving they pre-existed on the main branch and had nothing to do with this item's change. Every reviewer across multiple cycles who had called this "pre-existing, not caused by this diff" was correct; the deterministic check was comparing against a phantom baseline of zero instead of the true pre-existing count. Root cause: the retry instruction told the agent to defer to "the first round's baseline file, already on disk" with no verification such a file actually existed — when a baseline was never captured (because, e.g., the environment needed for it was unavailable on the ORIGINAL round too, so the step was then correctly skipped, but later became necessary), every subsequent retry kept honoring an exception that referred to nothing. Fix: the retry exception now opens with an explicit existence check — only when a real baseline file already exists does the "don't recapture" protection apply; when none exists, the agent is told to capture one regardless of retry status. |
| KI-E490 | **Ported from a host-mount session — defense-in-depth for the entry above, requested explicitly after an owner directive to generalize the fix rather than patch the one instance: even with the upstream prompt fix, the baseline-tolerance calculation could not distinguish a REAL zero (a baseline run genuinely found nothing pre-existing) from a PHANTOM zero (nothing was ever captured or reported) — a prompt instruction is not a guarantee an agent follows it on every item shape, and the override's own failure message presented both cases with identical, unqualified confidence.** Extracted the distinction into two new pure, independently-tested functions: one that returns true only when the effective baseline is zero AND neither a reported count nor a parsed on-disk baseline file backs it, and one that appends an explicit caveat to a failure message, but ONLY when that message actually names a "beyond baseline zero" comparison. The fold-time check now routes both its relevant failure paths through the annotator before writing the ledger note, so a human reading an escalation (or a future investigation of a repeat) sees "this zero is a DEFAULT, not a measurement" immediately, instead of needing to independently rediscover — as happened here, first-hand — that no baseline file ever existed before trusting a failure count at all. Deliberately scoped as a message-quality improvement, not a verdict change: a genuinely phantom baseline still fails the item exactly as before. |
| KI-E491 | **Ported from a host-mount session — a multi-step fixer step conflated "a build error MY OWN edit just introduced" with "a genuine external boundary I cannot cross," both routed through the same halt signal that stops the WHOLE item for a human decision, rather than just fixing the one-line mistake.** Live incident: a step added a new test method referencing a helper class without importing its namespace, then halted for an external-boundary reason — its own note even described the exact one-line fix needed, but it halted instead of writing that line. The item's own downstream triage, generating the human-decision queue entry, independently reached the same verdict a person would: "this is a mechanical fixer failure, not a genuine decision — re-queue with the exact import pinned." Root cause: the per-step halt instruction named the genuine grounds for an external-boundary halt but never said what does NOT qualify, leaving "I hit a compile error and don't want to finish" indistinguishable from "I would have to touch something outside my scope." Fix: the per-step prompt now states explicitly that the external-boundary halt is ONLY for a genuine documented red-line or a locked file, and NEVER for a build error the step's own edit introduced — that is incomplete work to finish in the same call, like any other compile error, not a boundary to halt on. |
| KI-E492 | **Ported from a host-mount session — a model-fit question (can the cheapest model actually be trusted for a new task class, and for a proposed consolidation of several checks into one call) was answered with a real, independent-model evaluation rather than a guess, before anything shipped on the strength of it.** Ran synthetic scenarios with known ground truth against the cheap-tier model, graded by multiple separately-launched top-tier evaluators who formed their own judgment from the code BEFORE looking at the cheap model's answer. An early round scored perfectly but one evaluator flagged a real gap: nothing tested "plausible-but-wrong" fixes (right symbols present, broken logic) — called "the dominant real-world false-positive class" and refused to clear the cheap model for unsupervised use without it. A follow-up round with harder traps (a guard placed after the vulnerable code path; a guard checking the wrong variable; inverted boolean logic) was caught correctly on all three, with a separate evaluator confirming while adding an honest caveat that the test doesn't yet fully discriminate genuine verification from matching against an over-obvious target. A separate test of CONSOLIDATING several independent checks into one merged call found zero measured quality degradation in this one sample, but the evaluator flagged two structural risks regardless of sample size: single-point-of-failure risk (one merged call failing is worse than one of several independent calls failing) and the untested direction that actually matters (a genuinely mixed true/false case, not yet tested at all). Action taken, following the evaluators' own recommendation: added a host-policy-gated, OFF-by-default, purely observational shadow probe that re-checks all axes together only when the real separate checks already passed, recording agreement/disagreement without ever affecting the real verdict — collecting real data toward a future promotion decision, never silently promoting the merge itself. |
| KI-E493 | **Ported from a host-mount session — an existing sandboxed-write-isolation mechanism's own documented validation only ever exercised ONE isolated call in isolation; nothing tested a SEQUENCE of isolation-wrapped calls against the SAME item, which is exactly a multi-step fixer's shape.** Live incident, the very next real batch after the gap above shipped: a trivial, correctly-planned multi-step mechanical fix got stuck with a nonsensical halt — the fixer's own summary claimed full completion while ALSO signaling an external-boundary halt, and the item's own triage correctly recognized this as fiction: "not a scope-stop — a phantom halt from a worktree mismatch; the claimed changes aren't actually present." Root-caused from the raw run log: each step's own isolation sandbox was a DIFFERENT, disconnected throwaway directory with zero memory of a prior step's edits — step 1's work stranded in one sandbox, step 2 starting over in a different one, and so on, so a multi-step item's cross-step continuity (the entire premise of the multi-step mechanism) silently broke the moment isolation was active, with each step's own self-report still claiming success (since it genuinely DID complete — just in the wrong tree) giving no visible signal anything was wrong. Not fixed at the root (the exact reason a per-call isolated sandbox doesn't persist across sequential calls to the same item is outside what could safely be introspected or redesigned without risking the isolation mechanism's own validated single-call behavior) — flagged as a real open question. What IS fixed: a new, zero-agent-call, purely deterministic check — a plain string-prefix filter against data already in memory, no extra token cost — wired into every call site that can produce a claimed-files list, catching an absolute path that is not rooted at the item's own real worktree as unambiguous proof of this exact divergence, and failing the item immediately and cheaply before wasting any further pipeline stage on data that was never real. |
| KI-E494 | **Ported from a host-mount session — a new shadow-probe policy (see the entry above) shipped inert: a config key added and flipped on, that never actually reached a real launch, because a separate, independent config loader has its own hardcoded allowlist, and the merge logic only ever copies keys already present in it.** Self-caught while preparing the very next real batch: the engine's own dry-run status line, which prints every effective policy, showed four flags where the configuration actually declared five. Traced to the loader's merge logic iterating only over its own hardcoded default-keys list — the new policy was never added to that list when it shipped, so the loader silently dropped it on every real launch; a test suite that injects policy values directly (bypassing the real config-file loading path entirely) structurally could never have caught this. Fix: added the missing key to the loader's default-keys object — the one place every enforcement layer already keys off, so one line fixed it everywhere at once. The generalizable lesson: test coverage of a new policy's EFFECT is necessary but not sufficient; it proves the flag does something once true, never that a live host can actually SET it to true through the real config-file path. Added a round-trip assertion (write a local config overlay, call the real loader, confirm the flag comes back true) as the missing integration check, with an explicit comment on the default-keys list for the next policy addition to find. |
| KI-E495 | **Ported from a host-mount session — after the entry above correctly detected the SAME item hit the same sandboxed-write-isolation mismatch a SECOND time (at the opposite end of a fresh multi-step plan), the pattern across both incidents pointed at the actual mechanism, not just a symptom to keep catching.** Both incidents' claimed-files paths were rooted EXACTLY at the agent's own isolation sandbox, never some unrelated location — the specific, consistent shape of "a write that resolves relative to wherever your tools currently are, not the absolute path you were told is yours." The existing isolation-hint text only ever described the OTHER half of this: a write to the truly-shared main checkout is rejected, loudly. It never said what happens here: a write that lands in your OWN isolated sandbox is not the shared checkout, so it is not rejected either — it just silently succeeds in the wrong place. Fix, two parts: (1) rewrote the isolation hint to say this explicitly and mandate checking every write path against the real worktree root both before and after use, not just "when you see a rejection." (2) Added a new, reusable one-bounded-retry mechanism, mirroring an established one-bounded-amend idiom elsewhere in the pipeline, wired into every call site that can hit this mismatch: on detection, the SAME role gets one more call with the SAME original instructions plus an explicit correction naming the exact wrong path and the exact required prefix, before the item ever fails. The item that surfaced this was, on inspection, already 90% complete from its earlier correctly-landed steps — the missing piece was added by hand as a one-time recovery, left uncommitted per the standing "a human commits real application code" rule. |
| KI-E496 | **Ported from a host-mount session — an owner directive asked for a GENERALIZABLE, AUTOMATED mechanism to finish items that are "almost done," immediately after a one-off manual rescue of an item that had most of its multi-step work land correctly and lost only the last step to the isolation mismatch above.** The gap: an existing "never reuse prior work on a retry" protection was a single on/off switch that disabled ALL reuse for ANY retry, conflating two genuinely different cases: (a) a whole-item fix that a REAL REVIEW GATE had rejected — resurrecting that is genuinely unsafe, since the artifact already failed adversarial scrutiny; (b) a multi-step sequence that halted PARTWAY THROUGH, before ever reaching a real gate — unreviewed, mechanically-verified-correct work that the blanket switch was discarding for a reason that never applied to it. Fix: a new function walks per-step artifacts sequentially from step 1, stopping at the first gap (missing file, halt signal, a step whose timestamp predates the current plan — proof it belongs to a stale, superseded plan), and re-proves each accepted step's claimed files against the LIVE worktree diff rather than trusting the step's own self-report. A second new function checks the item's own accumulated review-gate history and returns true only when NO real gate or review has EVER seen this item — a cheap pre-band probe doesn't count, since pre-band probes aren't the adversarial review the original protection existed to guard against. The retry-reuse path now fires only when BOTH conditions hold; anything that ever reached a real gate falls straight through to the original, completely unchanged, unconditional block. A genuine off-by-one was caught by the session's own new test before shipping: the initial resume-validity check wrongly rejected the legitimate "every step already done, only consolidation remains" case and fell back to a full cold restart instead of the intended zero-step resume. |
| KI-E497 | **Ported from a host-mount session — observed from static inspection of the mechanism above's own output on its first real production firing, before the batch even finished: it resumed from the wrong step, skipping fewer already-proven steps than expected.** Root-caused by reading every step artifact directly: one step's file used a completely different, self-invented field shape from every sibling step's shared shape, with neither of the two fields the resume-check logic actually looks for present — so the check correctly, per its own logic, treated a genuinely-correct step as an unproven halt. The step's actual work was independently confirmed correct by reading the live worktree diff directly. The real root cause one layer beneath: the pipeline's own execution script runs in a sandboxed runtime with NO filesystem access, so it cannot itself deterministically persist a step's own structured, schema-validated result to disk — the only way that artifact reaches disk at all is a SEPARATE prose instruction asking the same agent to freehand-write "your JSON" there as a second action, with nothing tying that freehand write to the schema-validated value the harness actually consumes in memory during the same run. The two channels can silently diverge, and this step simply invented its own progress-log vocabulary instead of mirroring the shape it had just satisfied moments earlier. Fix, two conservative parts, neither loosening the actual safety check: (1) tightened the write instruction to spell out the required field names verbatim and explain why a later cycle depends on them exactly. (2) Added a function recognizing the ONE synonym shape actually observed in the wild as an ADDITIONAL path into the SAME content-proof gate — the live worktree-diff proof still runs unchanged and is still the actual safety property; recognizing a wider set of "worth checking" shapes is not the same as trusting more content unchecked, and an explicit, narrow exact-match avoids sweeping in genuinely ambiguous or in-progress steps. |
| KI-E498 | **Ported from a host-mount session — after an owner directive that false completion claims from the implementation side are unacceptable, following a late-sequence step in a long multi-step fix that failed at fold time on a phantom claim.** Root-caused precisely: the final step's own artifact claimed creating a new test file, and its own captured build/test transcript shows this was NOT fabrication — real, named tests genuinely built and passed at some point during that call. The file was simply gone from the tracked worktree diff by the time anything checked again, consistent with a known, separately-documented tool-write inconsistency this same session had already found and partially worked around elsewhere. The mechanical gap: an existing phantom-claim probe only ever checked the FULL accumulated claim ONCE, at the very end of the whole multi-step sequence — so a phantom claim from an EARLY or MIDDLE step survives into every later step's own context, gets built on top of, and burns the entire rest of the run before the one-shot check at the end finally catches it, which is exactly what happened at the worst possible position: the LAST step, meaning the whole sequence's cost was already sunk. Fix: a new per-step phantom check, dispatched immediately after EACH step accepts and before its claim is trusted into the context later steps read — the same probe role and write-then-run pattern already established for the whole-item check, scoped to just that one step's claim. A phantom result gets ONE bounded same-step retry, mirroring an established idiom exactly: the same step is asked to redo the work, reading each claimed file back immediately after editing it before reporting success. If the retry still shows a phantom, the item fails immediately, attributed to the EXACT step responsible — before any later step, consolidation, or the gate band ever runs. The existing whole-item check at the end of the sequence is unchanged and stays the close authority regardless; this is a fail-fast optimization layered in front of it. |
| KI-E214 | **Ported from a host-mount session — pre-band probe dispatch parallelized for two provably-independent probe clusters, with two more genuine-parallelization candidates deliberately left sequential because their own test coverage slices the source code by comment-header position (a hoisted dispatch would silently relocate the content the test asserts on out from under it).** Cluster 1: a proof-marker probe and its own coverage-sibling probe now fire concurrently instead of one full round-trip at a time — neither reads the other's return value, and both gating conditions are fixed before either dispatches. Cluster 2: four independent read-only lints with no fixer-amend path now fire concurrently the same way. Both clusters preserve the exact original fail-fast priority order: results are awaited together, then walked in the original sequence, so the first one whose condition fails still wins with the identical failure message, before a later probe's bookkeeping ever runs — verified by re-running the full test suite before/after (byte-identical modulo timing jitter). The one deliberate behavioral difference: on an item where an earlier probe (in priority order) would have failed, the later probe(s) in its cluster now ALSO dispatch — extra cost on an already-failing item, traded for collapsing the passing-path latency. Two other candidate probes were specifically audited and left untouched because of the test-coverage-position risk; a separate existing short-circuit-before-another-probe contract was independently confirmed preserved byte-for-byte. The review-stage's many role gates and method-review flows, the disputed-gate adjudicator re-gate, and the refute+re-audit stages were audited (not assumed) and found already dispatched concurrently — no change needed there. |
| KI-E215 | **Ported from a host-mount session — a manual-recovery command crashed with an uncaught file-not-found error reading a test-stage artifact, when a FAILED item had genuinely completed test-authoring but that stage's OWN disk artifact never actually reached disk.** Root cause: the test-authoring role brief's write instruction is an unenforced prose side-instruction, separate from the SAME call's schema-required return payload the engine actually trusts in-memory — the engine records the artifact path unconditionally right after the call with no existence check, unlike a fixer's file-change claims, which ARE independently verified against the real worktree diff before anything downstream trusts them. An agent told to write a documentation-style side file can simply skip it and nothing catches it. Live evidence: two separate items both show a completed test-authoring stage in their attempt history, yet neither has the expected artifact file on disk. Fix: both the artifact read and a companion transcript read are now tolerant (missing/unparseable resolves to null, never a throw); a missing artifact first tries a best-effort derivation of candidate test files from sibling artifacts and the item's own declared file list, with the recovery contract's own cross-check against an independently-sourced proof transcript as the safety net against a wrong candidate; and the whole recovery-contract computation now runs inside a try block, so ANY failure degrades to an explicit, actionable manual-recovery message instead of an uncaught crash or a silently-empty automated contract. Does NOT fix the underlying unverified disk-write side effect itself — tracked as a separate, open follow-up. |
| KI-E216 | **Ported from a host-mount session — the manual-recovery fold path has NO attestation lane for a code item whose automated cryptographic evidence contract genuinely cannot be computed, discovered immediately downstream of the crash fix above.** Once the crash stopped, both affected items degraded to a manual-recovery path — but attempting to fold the resulting hand-verified evidence (a real build+test run, teed to a durable artifact, plus an independent fresh re-check agent's own confirmation) was rejected outright: "recovery verification contract missing." Root cause: the fold-time verification function throws immediately unless a specific contract object is present, which only gets set inside the fully-automated recovery branch — the manual-fallback branch deliberately leaves it unset, but the fold path has no OTHER way to accept a human-supervised recovery for a code-change item, unlike the NON-recovery fold path, which DOES accept a plain build/test transcript directly. A deeper, independent instance of the same class: one item's own proof transcript recorded its target as a project DIRECTORY rather than a specific project file, but the validation helper required a literal file — so even a fully-present, well-formed artifact agreeing with that transcript would ALSO have thrown, for an unrelated reason. Flagged as real follow-up, not silently worked around: (a) the directory-target validation gap, and (b) the missing human-supervised-attestation lane for recovery — mirroring an honest-blocker protocol this same engine already applies elsewhere. Until both land, the affected items' real, independently-verified fixes remain uncommitted on their own backup branches, folded to no automated verdict — a human reviews and commits them directly. |
| KI-E217 | **Ported from a host-mount session — fixed part (a) from the entry above (the directory-shaped proof-target validation gap); doing so unblocked one affected item's automated recovery contract far enough to expose two further, narrower, genuinely distinct gaps, documented rather than chased into a third layer.** Fix: the validation helper now resolves a proof/filter target through a new helper first — if the recorded path is a directory (normal: the test runner auto-discovers the single project file inside one, exactly what the build/test script's own subcommands already rely on), it locates the owning project file first, then validates THAT the same way an explicit file target always was. Verified: re-running recovery for the affected item now computes a real automated contract, previously always degraded to the manual-fallback message. Two further gaps this surfaced, not fixed here: (1) the recovery evidence contract locks to the ORIGINAL proof's exact test-method identity, not the underlying requirement it proved — the item's remedy legitimately restructured a single-case test into a parameterized one covering multiple cases, a genuine improvement, so the contract's derived filter no longer matches anything; the exact-string filter match has no notion of "this test was soundly restructured, not abandoned." (2) a separate, pre-existing test-result-aggregation gap: the real run is genuinely green end-to-end by the console's own count, but a parameterized test's generated display name collided with another test's identity somewhere in the full solution-wide run, so the structured counter reported the result as unavailable rather than green — not investigated further here, flagged as a separate, narrower, unrelated-to-correctness gap in the result-parsing logic. |
| KI-E218 | **A prior entry's gap — whole-solution `suite`-step TRX-aggregation failure during recovery — is not a one-off tied to one item's test parameterization; it recurred on a completely unrelated item/solution with a different specific TRX diagnostic.** Recovering that second item (independently re-checked APPROVED, real build 0 errors, real filter step passed) hit the mandatory `suite` step (recovery always demands FULL-band evidence — build+filter+suite — regardless of the item's own original band) against a multi-project solution: the console output was fully, genuinely green on both test projects, but `build-test.sh suite` still emitted a TRX diagnostic ("execution/definition mismatch") and exited nonzero — a different specific TRX-parser failure mode than the first incident's "ambiguous duplicate TRX test identity," on a solution with no parameterized tests at all. Common thread: `.sln`-level `dotnet test` against a multi-project solution (2+ test assemblies, or 1000+ tests in one) — the TRX aggregation logic has more than one reliability gap at this scale. Practical consequence: the recover/fold mechanism's mandatory `suite` requirement was substantially unreliable for any real multi-project solution at the time; both affected items' real, independently-re-checked-APPROVED fixes stayed on their own branches, uncommitted, pending direct human review, rather than risk an automated-but-unreliable close. Root-caused and fixed the next day (see following entry). |
| KI-E219 | **Root-caused and fixed both of the prior entry's real-world TRX-aggregation failure modes — both are legitimate, common xUnit/VSTest output shapes, not malformed TRX.** Reproduced directly against real solutions, raw TRX inspected by hand: (1) "ambiguous duplicate TRX test identity" — the VSTest adapter truncates long theory-argument `ToString()` output in generated display names, so two genuinely different tests (different internal test/execution ids, both independently unique) can render an identical VISIBLE display string — a coincidental display collision between two real, distinct tests, not a logical duplicate. (2) "TRX execution/definition mismatch" — a parameterized test whose data rows the adapter cannot stably serialize gets ONE shared test definition for ALL its data rows instead of one per row; each row still gets its own genuinely unique execution id, but the shared definition's single execution reference can only literally equal ONE of them, so the other rows necessarily "mismatch" a definition they legitimately share. Fix: (1) a colliding display-name identity is now disambiguated by folding the already-proven-unique definition id into the reported identity instead of throwing — used only for reporting/dedup, never for pass/fail counting, which reads the XML counters block directly. (2) the definition/execution check moved to a post-loop pass accepting a shared definition as long as its recorded execution id is genuinely one of the real execution ids reported against that same test id — a value matching none of them is still refused exactly as before. Verified end-to-end against the real solutions that originally triggered the bug, both now passing cleanly through the actual suite command. Added regression tests covering the accepted case, the still-refused tampering case, and the original strict single-result case. |
| KI-E220 | **The `suite` step never checked for real-infra evidence at all, unlike `filter` — fixing that exposed a deeper, third-party-tool limitation left honestly unresolved.** A real-infra-requiring item whose ONLY verification evidence comes from a whole-solution `suite` run (no separate `filter` invocation targeting the specific realinfra-touching test) could never surface a realinfra marker or satisfy the real-infra gate, even when a real container genuinely ran underneath. Fixed the narrow gap: `suite` now runs with detailed console logging and emits the realinfra marker exactly like `filter` already did. This did NOT fully solve the motivating case, for a reason worth recording honestly: confirmed by direct, isolated reproduction that the test runner's console logger reliably surfaces per-test realinfra markers only when test-collection parallelization is disabled; under default parallel execution, a large suite's own realinfra markers are silently dropped in the console-logger's relay, even though the underlying tests genuinely ran against real containers and passed. This is a property of the third-party test runner's own output-relay behavior under concurrency, not a bug in this engine's code. Deliberately NOT fixed by disabling suite-wide parallelization (a real performance regression for every solution's suite runs) without an explicit scope decision. Real follow-up options, none taken at the time: run the evidence-critical suite invocation non-parallel only when a realinfra marker is otherwise absent and the item needs one; or have realinfra-touching tests additionally write a marker FILE that survives regardless of console-relay behavior. |
| KI-E221 | **A test-artifact-provenance fallback's three sources could ALL be absent at once — root-caused and fixed with a 4th, more authoritative source: the live worktree diff.** An item kept degrading to a manual-fallback message even after a fresh, genuine RED proof was generated for it. Root cause: that item had neither a fix manifest nor a verify cross-check block at all — its original pipeline run reached the verify stage with a worktree byte-identical to HEAD (test-author and fixer never executed on that attempt), so none of the three candidate sources (graph file list, fix manifest, verify cross-check) had anything to read. The actual test suite was later built directly in the worktree by a subsequent pass — fully present on disk but invisible to any of the three JSON-sourced candidate lists. Fix: the fallback now takes the live worktree path and, only when the first three sources yield nothing, adds a 4th source — the actual worktree diff, filtered to test-shaped paths exactly like the other three. Strictly more authoritative (reflects what genuinely landed on disk regardless of whether any JSON note was ever written) and stays behind the same independent cross-check every source already goes through — a wrong guess still fails loudly, never silently. Verified: re-running recovery on the motivating item now computes a real evidence contract instead of degrading to the manual scaffold. |
| KI-E222 | **Fixed a bash-variable-capture bug directly in the build/test runner's `filter` and `suite` cases — general, no performance tradeoff, no invocation-shape change for any caller.** Both cases previously captured the test runner's combined stdout/stderr via a bash variable, then repeatedly re-processed that ONE variable with multiple separate greps. Confirmed live that this reproducibly loses data for a multi-project solution target under concurrent test-runner child-process writers — the exit code and TRX-derived counts stay correct, but the console text carrying an out-of-band marker gets silently reduced to a small fraction of its real size. A second, narrower gotcha survived even after switching to reading from a file: a direct grep against the variable still failed to extract a match despite a presence-only grep on the SAME variable correctly reporting one present. Fix: both cases now redirect the test runner's output directly to a scratch file instead of a bash variable; the marker-extraction and summary logic all read from the file too — nothing round-trips through a shell variable anymore. Verified end-to-end against the real motivating solution with its normal invocation shape (default parallelism, no workaround): the out-of-band marker now surfaces correctly. `build` and the RED-capture path were deliberately left untouched — no evidence either has the same defect profile. |
| KI-E223 | **A recovery scaffold was unfoldable for a BLOCKED- or ESCALATED-origin item until an operator manually ran a reset command first — a step only mentioned in prose, with no enforcement.** The recovery-fold this function builds always starts its transitions at CLAIMED (the uniform "prove the fix cleanly from scratch" contract), but the state machine only allows a direct transition to CLAIMED from FAILED/CONFLICT/READY — a BLOCKED- or ESCALATED-origin item's otherwise evidence-complete, gate-approved fold was rejected outright at fold time with a confusing bad-transition error. Fix: the recovery-scaffold command now auto-resets a BLOCKED/ESCALATED row to READY (the one edge the state machine allows from both) as the first thing it does after confirming the item is recovery-eligible, reusing the existing, already-trusted transition primitive — logged to the console so an operator sees the state change, and aborting the scaffold rather than silently proceeding if the reset itself fails. The origin state's own history record is preserved, not erased — only the live state moves. |
| KI-E224 | **A verification-target-derivation helper crashed the scheduler's dry-run/group command with an uncaught throw for any item whose file list includes a cross-cutting infra/config file with no dotnet-build ownership anywhere in its ancestry — a real, previously-working item shape, not a hypothetical.** A shared Kubernetes manifest, monitoring-rule file, or environment-secrets file sits outside every service's own directory tree by design and will never resolve via a solutions map, a project-file directory-walk, or a target-prefix fallback, no matter how configuration is tuned. Confirmed this was a regression, not a missing one-time config step: several already-closed items had touched files in this exact shape successfully before this function's strict-throw form existed. Fix: when a file exhausts every existing resolution strategy — which, by construction, is real evidence the file isn't part of any dotnet-buildable tree — it is now silently skipped instead of throwing, exactly like the pre-existing non-code-file early exit one line above it. This does NOT weaken the two genuinely-ambiguous-multiple-candidates throws elsewhere in the function; only the "found nothing anywhere" case changed. Added a direct regression test covering both the skip and that the unrelated ambiguous-file throw still fires unchanged. |
| KI-E225 | **A phantom-manifest check false-failed a step where the fixer genuinely created the claimed file, because the probe reported it phantom anyway.** Root cause, confirmed by direct reproduction: the VCS status output collapses a wholly new untracked directory into ONE line naming the directory itself, never the files inside it — the diff-reading helper passes that line straight through as a real changed-path entry. A claimed file living INSIDE a brand-new directory then compares against a real entry that is SHORTER than the claim (the bare directory path) — neither existing suffix-match check can ever match when the real entry is the shorter, directory-only string, so every such claim reported phantom even though the diff genuinely, entirely contains it. A sibling test-artifact-provenance helper already special-cased this exact VCS behavior by recursing into a trailing-slash entry; this was the identical VCS behavior reaching a different, pure function with no filesystem access of its own. Fix: added a directory-prefix match — any claimed path inside a VCS-reported wholly-new directory is, by construction, part of the diff; no recursion needed. Confirmed this is the SAME function the live, in-pipeline probe every fixer/test-author step actually invokes imports, so one fix closed both the fold-time check and the live per-step probe. Verified end-to-end by reproducing the exact VCS behavior in a scratch repo and confirming before/after. |
