| KI-E226 | **A documented "even Bash is refused" recovery fallback actually corrupted the main tree this time — live, direct transcript evidence, not inference.** An item's fixer hit the exact scenario (Edit rejected for its OWN assigned worktree; Bash heredoc also refused) and used the documented "script via scratchpad" fallback — but the write's actual destination landed in the shared main checkout instead of the assigned worktree, silently, with no error. Caught only afterward by the main-tree-drift probe, which correctly failed the lane before spending the gate band — the same run also independently leaked a test file from a different item's worktree via the same unguarded-Bash-write class (a pre-existing, disclosed gap: a filesystem-level write-isolation guard is specific to the Edit/Write TOOLS, not a general sandbox — Bash writes to any path, including the shared checkout, are unaffected by it). The existing fallback guidance's own safety check ("read the file back afterward") does NOT catch this failure mode: the intended path's content can be perfectly correct while a SEPARATE staging/copy step's destination argument was ALSO wrong, landing a second copy in the shared checkout. This is prompt-level hardening, not a mechanical fix — no code-level guarantee exists for Bash writes. Added to the relevant prompt block: write directly to the final absolute path in ONE command for any such fallback, never stage into a scratchpad/temp file first; and an immediate self-check right after any such fallback write that explicitly instructs the agent to stop and disclose prominently if it ever detects the shared checkout was touched. The main-tree files this incident actually produced were deleted from the shared checkout by the operator; the authoritative content lives on the item's own worktree branch. |
| KI-E227 | **4 of 6 pre-band "bounded amend" call sites could silently misreport an INFRA failure (the amend call returning null after retries) as a real, evidenced CONTENT failure — the exact "infra-failure ≠ quality failure" distinction every OTHER agent-unavailable check already makes, missing here.** Live-caught: an item hit a mid-run session login expiry; its amend call returned unavailable 3x in a row (retries exhausted), and the code fell through every amend-outcome guard untouched, reaching the ORIGINAL pre-amend probe's stale gaps and reporting a real, specific-sounding content verdict for an amend that never actually got a chance to run. The infra-suspect flag was never set, so the fold-time auto-infra-retry (which does not burn the item's attempt count) never applied — the item was charged a real attempt for a login outage. Audited all 6 amend call sites for the same gap: 4 affected (all 4 short-circuit straight to a hard fail off the amend's outcome); 2 unaffected (one amend only feeds a later gate band's own judgment, never a pre-band auto-fail; another is advisory-only and never fails the item at all). Fix: added an explicit infra-suspect-and-fail guard immediately after each of the 4 affected calls — the same idiom already used for other role calls elsewhere in the same file. Confirmed an alternate-runtime port of this engine has no equivalent gap, since its architecture requires every amend to arrive as a real, schema-validated submission with no internal "retry silently then give up with null" concept to mirror. |
| KI-E228 | **A hand-run fold of an ESCALATED- or BLOCKED-origin recovery file rejects with a wall of confusing bad-transition lines unless a reset command ran first — a prior fix addressed this for the automated recovery-scaffold command, but a controller session re-running an EXISTING recovery-fold file (e.g. resuming from a handoff note) had no such auto-reset and hit the identical wall.** Resolved by running the reset command (ESCALATED/BLOCKED → READY, preserving claim/run identity and history) then re-folding the same file, which applied cleanly. Fix: the fold command's own summary now scans its rejected results for bad-transition entries whose target is CLAIMED and whose origin is BLOCKED/ESCALATED, and prints a hint naming the exact reset command and the result-identity-bump escape hatch needed to re-submit — message-only, no state-machine change. |
| KI-E229 | **A recovery-scaffold command's target-derivation threw "no trusted build/suite target" for a code-recovery item whose ENTIRE file list is non-code (a pure infra/doc/config finding), because an internal solution-fallback field was never given the same fallback a sibling helper one line above it already computed.** Root cause: the target-derivation step computes a solution fallback ONLY as an argument to one helper call — the SAME fallback is never threaded into the item object passed to the verification-expectations helper, whose own internal fallback reads that field directly, which no graph entry in this project ever populates — so the fallback was permanently dead for exactly the item class that needs it most (an infra-only finding has no code file to derive a project from in the first place). Fix: compute the fallback once and pass it through both call sites via a shallow merge. Live-verified end-to-end: after the fix, recovering a real item with a pure non-code file list successfully computed a full build/filter/suite contract and printed real, runnable commands instead of degrading to the manual-recovery message. |
| KI-E230 | **A resolved owner ruling does NOT unblock an item on its own — the scheduler's eligibility check ALSO excludes any item whose fix-type classification is still literally "owner-decision," and applying a ruling never updates that classification — so a batch of owner rulings can sit fully resolved-in-the-graph yet permanently invisible to scheduling, with NO error, warning, or ledger-state signal that anything is wrong.** Root cause: the eligibility guard is two INDEPENDENT conditions ORed into one exclusion — before a ruling, both correctly fire; after a ruling, only the resolved-flag flips to true, since nothing in the codebase ever reclassifies the fix-type away from "owner-decision," so the second condition keeps firing forever regardless of the first. Found while investigating why the scheduler showed almost nothing schedulable despite hundreds of nominal READY items: a new audit check found over a dozen items across many services carrying a genuine, specific, dated ruling that had been completely inert for weeks — some visibly stuck, others sitting in a healthy-looking ledger state yet silently invisible to scheduling, indistinguishable from a genuinely-competing-priority item. Fix (that session, hand-verified, not yet a fully automated code fix): added a new advisory audit check flagging every item where the resolved-flag is true but the fix-type is still "owner-decision" — report-only, never auto-rewritten, since the correct replacement classification is a judgment call from reading the ruling text, not mechanically derivable — then hand-reclassified every flagged item after reading its ruling. Flagged, not silently absorbed: a wider parked-decision backlog was not individually re-verified in the same pass, only the items the new audit could mechanically surface; a genuine code fix (auto-transition the classification at the moment the ruling is resolved) would close this class permanently instead of relying on the advisory audit being re-run and hand-actioned. |
| KI-E231 | **A cost-band triage helper checked cluster-size sweep-eligibility BEFORE severity, so a CRITICAL or HIGH finding that happened to share title vocabulary with several other services' findings was silently routed to the cheap bulk-sweep band instead of the full-review band — exactly backwards from the stated intent to reserve full review for the genuinely critical items, and dangerous if "run the sweeps first" advice were followed literally without independently re-auditing every claimed sweep site's severity first.** Root cause: the cluster-size check ran BEFORE the severity check, and that existing severity check only ever covered CRITICAL, never HIGH, even though every review role in the project treats CRITICAL and HIGH as the same blocking tier. Found while investigating effectiveness on a live, large item graph: one large cluster sharing common vocabulary was entirely sweep-banded despite being deeply heterogeneous, service-specific security defects that share vocabulary but NOT a mechanical fix shape — the textbook case a bulk sweep would get badly wrong. Fix: reordered the triage so (severity CRITICAL OR HIGH) AND theme-in-full-review-set is checked FIRST, before the sweep-size check — widened from CRITICAL-only to CRITICAL-or-HIGH to match the rest of the project's severity-tier convention. Defense in depth: the sweep-claim command no longer trusts a possibly-stale sweep-spec file alone for this; it independently re-derives the identical gate at claim time and refuses to claim any site that fails it, regardless of what the spec file says. Verified impact on the live graph: re-running the clustering step post-fix roughly doubled the full-review-band item count and cut the sweep-band count correspondingly — the honest cost of doing these findings right, not an error. |
| KI-E232 | **A scope-stop item (an owner-gated hard boundary) can be re-claimed and re-verified cycle after cycle with an UNCHANGED worktree, paying a full verification cycle each time to re-derive the exact same "still not fixed" outcome — no mechanism ever flagged "nothing changed since last time" before spending another slot.** An item sat blocked for weeks on a single silently-reverted file, got reset as part of an unrelated investigation, then was re-claimed and re-verified — the verify step's own runner surfaced an unprompted observation pointing out that its own dispatch mechanically could not have changed the outcome, since nothing upstream had changed since the prior blocked cycle, and recommended exactly this check. Fix: a new advisory audit check that, for every scope-stop item with an on-disk worktree, computes a content signature over the current byte-content of every file in its file list (a missing file hashes to a stable marker so a deletion is itself a detected change) and compares it against a sidecar captured on the PRIOR audit run. A signature match surfaces as a named staleness flag in the audit report. Deliberately advisory-only, never exclusionary — this does not remove the item from scheduling eligibility, since "is the ruling still pending, or did the operator just fix it and hasn't re-run yet" is not mechanically derivable — it only makes a repeat-without-progress pattern visible BEFORE another slot is spent. A CLOSED item is skipped entirely, so a stale sidecar left behind by an item that later gets fixed and folded produces no false report once it closes. |
| KI-E234 | **A recovery README's evidence-contract commands are scaffolded with absolute worktree-under-repo-root paths, but nothing stops (or clearly flags) a controller running them with the equivalent repo-root-relative path instead — the containment helper treats a relative target as relative-TO-THE-WORKTREE, so a repo-root-relative string that happens to already start with the worktree's own path prefix resolves to a doubled, nonsensical, nonexistent path, and the resulting file-not-found error gives zero hint that "use an absolute path" is the fix.** Hit this firsthand during a real direct-recovery, despite an existing memory note already warning to use the README's exact absolute-path commands verbatim — the relative-path convention used everywhere else for driver commands made it easy to slip into the same convention for the build/test target arguments by muscle memory. Compounded by a SECOND instance of the same class one level down: a captured pre-fix baseline using the SAME wrong relative-path convention still "worked" in isolation because BOTH sides of a later comparison were wrong the same way — only surfaced once the official evidence transcript was corrected to absolute paths and the two no longer matched. Flagged, not silently absorbed: the ready-to-implement fix is to have the build/test runner itself resolve and echo an ABSOLUTE path into every start marker regardless of how its own target argument was spelled — closing the whole class at the source instead of relying on every future controller session remembering a specific instruction under pressure. Until implemented, the workaround is exactly what the README already says: copy its absolute-path commands verbatim, never substitute a repo-root-relative equivalent. |
| KI-E235 | **A documented engine-sync event: this host mount rebased onto the public upstream engine branch after the two had diverged BOTH ways — the host mount carried roughly 50+ numbered fixes upstream never received, while upstream had added an entire new subsystem (artifact-path-safety guards, cost-basis accounting, cross-runtime portable-evidence hashing, review-decision normalization, native-persistence/checkpoint infrastructure) the host mount lacked.** Merged file-by-file across the engine's library modules, driver, pipeline script, role briefs, config/setup/verify files, and the alternate-runtime port — using upstream as the base and re-applying every host-local feature on top, EXCEPT for the driver and pipeline-script files specifically, where the host-local version was kept as the base instead, since both turned out to be strict supersets of upstream's own pipeline functionality (several entire pipeline stages the host side had built did not exist in upstream's branch at all). Three real bugs found and fixed along the way, all confirmed pre-existing in vendored/upstream code, none caused by the merge itself: (1) a path-safety guard rejected every path under certain system temp directories unconditionally on one OS family, because its ancestor-walk treats ANY symlink as unsafe and that OS ships static symlinks into those exact locations — fixed with a narrow allowlist trusting only a symlink whose target is the expected OS-specific real path; (2) a text-anchor-based locator in a prompt-contract self-test assumed a specific function ordering that this host's pipeline script has never actually had — reverted the anchor to the correct adjacent function name; (3) an environment-provisioning test hung and then failed on unrelated stray output, because the host's own self-test had no multi-suite dispatch handling before this sync, so a spawned targeted-suite child ran the FULL hand-rolled suite instead of the intended narrow check. Two items deliberately deferred, not silently dropped, each independently confirmed as touching the SAME not-yet-integrated subsystem: (a) a driver↔runtime cryptographic contract-binding / portable-evidence architecture is only partially wired into this host's own dispatch pipeline, leaving a cluster of focused-suite tests failing at the same root cause (a test harness expecting a contract shape the partially-adopted subsystem does not yet fully produce); (b) a small terminology mismatch between two internal naming conventions ("admission" vs "checkpoint") was proven pre-existing and unrelated to this sync specifically. Also fixed: a stale documentation table of expected physical-invocation counts, re-derived by actually executing the real fixture rather than hand-computed; roughly a dozen stale self-test assertions describing wording an upstream rewrite had legitimately changed (substance verified intact each time before updating assertion text); a few parity drifts between the pipeline script and its alternate-runtime port's own mirrored wording; one corrupted (mid-sentence-truncated) table row restored from upstream's complete version. |
| KI-E236 | **Follow-up scope opened by the prior engine-sync entry, not yet started: fully integrate the portable-evidence / driver↔runtime cryptographic contract-binding subsystem into the pipeline script's actual dispatch flow, and reconcile a terminology split between two internal naming conventions ("admission" vs "checkpoint").** Both are pre-existing/newly-vendored gaps from the sync, not regressions. Not attempted in the same change, per this project's own convention that a fix this size deserves its own focused pass with fresh reasoning about the actual contract, not a guess bolted onto an already-large sync. |
| KI-E237 | **A new pre-band probe + gate-prompt mandate closing a session's single most-repeated failure theme: fixes for race conditions kept introducing NEW races.** Several unrelated items' fixes each either left a new race behind or had their own regression test flake under concurrency. Individual reviewers had been running the affected suite a double-digit number of times, inconsistently, per their own ad hoc judgment. A new probe detects (git-diff-only, no LLM) an added lock primitive, change-tracking call, or a process/task/parallel-construct specifically inside a test-project file (a path-segment check matching the project's real test-folder convention, not a naive regex). Wired as a pre-band probe; when it fires, the prompt-composition step injects a mandatory "run this 15+ times before approving, report the exact tally" notice into the four review roles most likely to independently verify runtime behavior — mirroring an existing repo-profile-injection mechanism's own shape (a mandate injected into prompts, not a pass/fail gate). The alternate-runtime port honestly declares this probe unported (a real gap, not silently dropped), with its schema mirrored for parity. Verified: real red→green on 3 self-found bugs during construction, confirmed via genuine execution. |
| KI-E238 | **A new pre-band probe catching a test method whose only reachable assertion sits behind a conditional early return, so it passes unconditionally regardless of whether the fix under test is applied.** Real incidents closed: one item's 4 test methods silently no-op'd when a file path couldn't be located, producing a false-green in a real CI job; another item's regression test scanned for the wrong interface entirely and could never fail on any code state. The scanner locates brace-balanced, string/comment-safe test-method bodies and flags one with zero assertion calls anywhere, or a conditional early return preceding its own last assertion — an explicit, sanctioned environment-dependent-skip idiom exempts the whole method. Wired into the build/test runner following an existing lint-wrapper's exact CLI shape. Correction (same session, caught by an actual live run): the tool was built and CLI-wired but never DISPATCHED by the pipeline script at all — a live batch completed its whole pre-band lint group with no corresponding artifact, proving the gap directly. Fixed by adding the dispatch call to the existing pre-band lint group (unconditional on any code change) — a genuine hit now fails the item pre-band, cheaply, since (unlike the concurrency probe) a vacuous test is an unambiguous defect needing no judgment call. |
| KI-E239 | **A convergence-bonus refinement — a second, independent grant path recognizing "primary gates clean, review-flow roles converging on one corroborated finding" as bonus-eligible on the very FIRST dissenting round**, extending an existing mechanism that structurally cannot fire before a second round exists to compare against. Real incidents addressed: one item where the primary gates approved but every review-flow role dissented in unison on ONE narrow finding, with retry attempts exhausted before a corrected round could complete; another where 3 of 6 review roles found the exact same small finding after the other 3 had already approved, needing a full extra round. A new corroboration heuristic (conservative, stopword-filtered title-word overlap) fires only when every primary gate role present is approved (fail-closed on a null verdict), 2+ review/editorial roles dissented, each reported EXACTLY ONE finding, and at least two of those single findings corroborate each other — deliberately narrow: a lone uncorroborated dissenter never qualifies, a dissenting primary gate disqualifies the path entirely, and a reviewer with multiple findings is excluded even if one would otherwise corroborate. |
| KI-E240 | **A new, on-demand, read-only integrity-sweep subcommand that re-checks a CLOSED item's current worktree content against the content-identity hash recorded at the moment its fold actually closed it, catching a class of incident every EXISTING drift check structurally cannot: a gate-approved fix silently reverting on disk with nobody currently watching.** Real incident: a 2-line fix, gate-approved, had its one file silently revert to pre-fix content while dozens of sibling files from the same original fix stayed correct, and sat blocked for over a month with zero owner-facing signal, because every existing drift check only watches an ACTIVE claim-time/batch-time window, never re-checking a CLOSED item later. No new capture mechanism was needed — every fold already records an aggregate whole-worktree content-identity hash at close time; the new sweep re-derives that same aggregate hash against the CURRENT worktree and compares it, naming the recorded evidence for a human to diff on a mismatch, and skipping a gone/garbage-collected worktree gracefully rather than crashing. Verified with a genuine red→green reproduction: temporarily reverted one real line in an already-closed worktree, confirmed the sweep correctly reported the drift naming the close date and evidence path, restored it, confirmed clean again. |
| KI-E241 | **A new pre-band probe running a real external shell-script linter on any `.sh` file a diff touches, following an existing precedent for shelling out to a real tool when text-diffing cannot answer the question.** A real incident's multi-round defect-discovery chain was mostly pure shell-scripting pitfalls — an error-exit guard suppressed under a conditional masking a real downstream failure behind a function's own trailing success echo; a background process racing the following execution of the same file. Empirically verified BEFORE shipping, matching the lesson to never assume a tool's behavior without checking it: the linter's DEFAULT check set does NOT catch this masking-bug class at all — the relevant check is an OPTIONAL one, only emitted with an "enable all checks" flag, at low severity by default. The classifier marks that specific check as a "notable, incident-cited" finding distinct from ordinary style noise, and gracefully reports "unavailable" (never crashes) when the linter binary itself is absent. End-to-end proof against the real incident: ran against the actual, untouched, already-closed worktree script and correctly flagged the exact masking-bug line as the one notable finding among dozens of ordinary style nits. Correction (same session, caught alongside a sibling probe's identical gap): likewise built and wired into the build/test runner but never dispatched by the pipeline script — fixed by adding the dispatch call gated on the diff naming a `.sh` path, failing the item pre-band only on a notable finding; ordinary style findings stay advisory. |
| KI-E242 | **A pre-band plan-feasibility probe's own instructions told it to skip the existence check entirely unless the plan's own text says a file is being newly created — so a step's "this needs to be added" premise was NEVER verified against the real worktree, only a claimed-EXISTING fact was.** Found by actually launching a real batch to test several other same-session improvements: one item's plan said "add file X defining four specific things" — the plan-review probe approved it clean on the first pass — but that exact file already existed, complete with all four things, when the fixer actually reached that step; a phantom-manifest check correctly caught the resulting "claimed a file change, zero corresponding diff" mismatch and failed the item, but only AFTER a full test-author + fixer dispatch had already been paid for — exactly the cost the feasibility probe exists to avoid, just triggered from the opposite direction of its own only-checked case. Fix: removed the blanket "newly created" exemption; the probe's prompt now explicitly checks BOTH directions — a step naming existing code is checked as before, AND a step naming something being newly added is now actually read/grepped for FIRST, flagging the premise as false if it's already fully present with the same effective content the step describes adding. |
| KI-E243 | **A write-isolation incident from a DIFFERENT trigger than two prior entries on the same theme — no write was ever rejected this time.** Found by relaunching an item specifically to test the prior entry's fix: the fixer's own native session started inside a full, genuine git checkout of its own under a harness-managed sandbox-worktree path — a mechanism a prior entry already names — carrying the harness's own git-safety notice telling the agent not to `cd` out of it. Confirmed independently that this is a long-standing, not new, platform behavior with many such directories accumulated over time. Rather than treating the notice as confirmation to double down on an existing worktree-path self-check, the fixer reasoned about "which worktree am I actually in" and adapted its OWN build/test invocation to the sandbox instead. Result: a complete, correct, individually-verified multi-file fix landed entirely inside the sandbox — none of it corrupt or hallucinated — while the item's actual assigned worktree (the only path the fold/verify/gate stages ever read) stayed almost empty; the item failed on a phantom-manifest claim for real work that simply never reached the right address. Not a first occurrence: an earlier item's own fix-manifest history shows the identical pattern, which nonetheless reached CLOSED, confirming this class is recoverable, not a silent data-loss risk, when caught. Fix (prompt-level hardening, matching an established posture — a full mechanical guarantee is outside this engine's control): the sandbox-worktree notice is explicitly named as real and correct for GIT SAFETY only, never as license to relocate the deliverable; "adapting around wherever your tools currently sit" is named explicitly as the failure mode, not a valid workaround; the worktree-path rule is restated as unconditional regardless of the agent's own starting directory or any harness notice. |
| KI-E244 | **KI-E498's own same-step retry prompt tells the fixer that an honest empty `filesChanged` is the CORRECT self-correction ("explanation (b)... Simply DROP this path... A step that genuinely changed nothing this turn should report an EMPTY filesChanged") — but the code that reads the retry response required `retried.filesChanged.length` to be truthy before even attempting recovery, so following that exact instruction produced `recovered = null` and an automatic FAILED.** Live incident, found by actually launching the diversified batch prepared to test KI-E237–E243 in production: a compliance-themed item step 3/8's first attempt evidently listed `HealthReadyRealInfraTests.cs` in `filesChanged` because it had just READ/verified the file (already complete from a prior attempt) while writing its "ready for step 4" summary — the initial per-step phantom probe correctly caught this as phantom (0 diff) and fired the retry; the retry response, per `state/items/a compliance-themed item/fix-step-3.json`, correctly returned `filesChanged: []` with a clear "satisfied by prior attempt" summary — a textbook-correct application of the retry prompt's own explanation (b). The gating code at the KI-E498 retry call site nonetheless required `retried.filesChanged && retried.filesChanged.length` before attempting the recheck-probe path at all; an empty array fails that condition, so `recovered` stayed `null` and the item failed with "claimed 1 file(s) with NO corresponding change... even after one same-step retry" — misreporting a *zero*-file claim as a repeated phantom claim. **Fix:** the retry-handling branch in `factory.js` (the same call site KI-E498 added) now checks `retried.filesChanged` for emptiness FIRST — an empty array (after the existing `outOfTreePaths` sanity check, which trivially passes on `[]`) is accepted as `recovered = retried` immediately, with no recheck-probe dispatch needed (an empty array cannot be phantom by definition, so there is nothing left to verify). A non-empty retry claim still goes through the original recheck-probe path unchanged. Verified: `outOfTreePaths(paths, wtPath)` (`factory.js:584`) is `(paths \|\| []).filter(...)`, confirmed to return `[]` (never throw) on an empty/undefined input, so the new empty-array branch cannot regress the existing out-of-tree guard. |

| KI-E247 | **A previously-recovered item's doc file silently reverted TWO unrelated, already-completed pieces of work — not a factory-engine defect this time, a data-integrity gap in the [[feedback-factory-recovery-no-redo]]/KI-E243 recovery method itself.** Live incident, found while auditing why a messaging-themed item (ESCALATED earlier today after exhausting 3 attempts) actually failed its final attempt: the reported cause was "fix introduced 1 new test failure(s): `MessagingServiceDocAccuracyTests.DataFlowDoc_DocumentsDeleteMessageRoute`" — investigating the actual diff showed the worktree's uncommitted `doc/data-flows/a messaging service.md` had REMOVED the `DELETE /api/messages/{id}` row (documenting ITEM-X's work) and the `PATCH /api/conversations/{id}` row (also ITEM-X), AND ITEM-X an entire `CustomerConsentRecordsController` section (endpoints, events, audit mapping) that ITEM-X had deliberately, permanently removed as an inert consumer-less feature — while the worktree's OWN committed HEAD (`20fdcb915`) correctly had all three pieces of the true history. Only a messaging-themed item's own genuine contribution (a new "§10 Observability — custom metrics" section) was legitimate; everything else in the diff was a reversion of unrelated, already-shipped work with no relationship to a messaging-themed item's own acceptance criteria. Circumstantial but strong evidence points to the KI-E243 recovery performed earlier the same day (a plain `cp` of 9 files, including this exact doc path, from the sandbox worktree `.claude/worktrees/wf_d0a2abb5-6fe-3` into the real `state/worktrees/a messaging-themed item`) as the mechanism: at the time of that recovery the sandbox's own copy of this file lacked the DELETE row (confirmed by direct read before the sandbox was reclaimed mid-investigation, consistent with the sandbox having branched from an earlier point than the real worktree's `20fdcb915` HEAD) — a blind file-level `cp` across two independently-created worktrees copies whatever content sits at the SOURCE path with no awareness that the two trees may have diverged on BASE commit, not just on the item's own intended edit. **This is the sharp edge [[feedback-factory-recovery-no-redo]]'s "recover only the missing stage, never blindly re-group" guidance did not yet cover**: recovering FILE CONTENT via `cp` needs the same "don't blindly redo/revert" discipline as recovering PIPELINE STAGE state — a plain copy is safe ONLY when the source and destination trees share the same base, and unsafe (silently regresses real work) when they do not, with no error or warning at copy time. **Fix applied to a messaging-themed item's worktree (this pass, not yet a mechanized guard):** reconstructed the doc file as `git show HEAD:<path>` (the verified-correct committed base) plus ONLY a messaging-themed item's own new §10 section appended, verified via targeted greps (DELETE row present, ITEM-X retraction note present as history, new metrics section present, no active consent-records section re-added) and a diff-stat confirming zero removed lines remain. **Not yet fixed at the mechanism level** — a future `cp`-based cross-worktree recovery should diff the two trees' base commits first (`git merge-base --is-ancestor` in both directions) and, on divergence, fall back to a per-hunk `git diff \| git apply`-style reconciliation (or at minimum flag the divergent files for manual review) rather than a blind file copy; this is scoped as follow-up, not implemented in this pass, since the immediate live corruption needed fixing first and a general-purpose recovery-reconciliation tool is a larger, separate piece of work. |

| KI-E249 | **Owner directive (2026-09-25): incorporate real BMAD skills/agents into the factory as a complement, "as many places as possible," with multiple agents doing the work.** Two things established by direct investigation before any code changed: (1) no live BMAD tool is reachable from this host at all — no package anywhere on the machine, no global npm install, no `node_modules` (confirmed via `find` + `npm ls -g` + `which bmad`) — so a literal "invoke the BMAD architect AGENT" call (the stateful "Winston"/"Amelia"/"Murat"/"John" personas under `.claude/skills/bmad-agent-*`/`bmad-tea`, which explicitly "do not break character until the user dismisses" them) cannot mean a full interactive session; (2) the factory had ALREADY done exactly this integration successfully, twice — five existing role briefs (`review-edgecase.md`/`review-adversarial.md`/`review-testreview.md`/`review-editorial-structure.md`/`review-editorial-prose.md`) are explicitly headed "BMAD `bmad-*` method — HEADLESS METHOD CARD," citing the specific skill each embeds. This means a dispatched factory agent can genuinely invoke the REAL, CURRENT skill content at runtime rather than rely on a hand-copied, driftable paraphrase. **Fix:** (1) added a `### BMAD skill` block to all five gates (`gate-architect.md`←`bmad-agent-architect`/Winston, `gate-developer.md`←`bmad-agent-dev`/Amelia, `gate-qa.md`←`bmad-tea`/Murat, `gate-po.md`←`bmad-agent-pm`/John, matching `dataflow.md`'s own "the `bmad-agent-pm` John persona acting as Product Owner" mapping) instructing the dispatched agent to invoke the real skill, explicitly skip its greeting/menu/wait-for-input steps (persona skills have them; this pipeline is single-shot), and apply the loaded persona's principles to the diff — plus one genuinely new checklist item per gate pulled from the real skill/its companion checklist (Winston: name trade-offs, not just verdicts; Amelia, from `bmad-dev-story/checklist.md`'s Definition-of-Done: only in-scope dependencies; Murat: flakiness is critical tech debt, plus Reliability/Maintainability NFR framing; John: read `acceptance` like a PRD requirement). `gate-security.md` got an honest "no dedicated BMAD security skill exists" note instead of a forced citation — direct inspection of `bmad-code-review` (the skill this repo's own root `dataflow.md` names for Security) shows it is an ORCHESTRATOR of `bmad-review-adversarial-general` + `bmad-review-edge-case-hunter` (both already live-invoked elsewhere in this pipeline) plus an Acceptance Auditor (already the factory's own `acceptance-scan` probe) — not a distinct lens with unincorporated content. (2) Upgraded all five EXISTING headless-method-card roles with a "Live drift check" note: invoke the real skill once to confirm the embedded protocol hasn't drifted since it was ported, preferring the newer skill text for anything that doesn't conflict with the role's own documented factory-specific deviation (e.g. review-adversarial's deliberate no-quota override stays absolute regardless). (3) Added a genuinely NEW stage, `review-nfr.md` (`bmad-testarch-nfr`/Murat's NFR-audit companion skill) — scoped to the two NFR categories nothing else in the pipeline owns (Performance, Reliability/Observability; Security is gate-security's job, Maintainability is gate-architect/gate-developer's — covering them again would be redundancy, not signal), advisory-only (never blocks on a missing/UNKNOWN performance budget alone, matching the real skill's own threshold-or-UNKNOWN discipline; only a genuinely evidenced CRITICAL/HIGH like a silently-swallowed failure counts), dispatched for every code item via a new `band: 'nfr'` entry in `flowsFor()` (factory.js) mirroring the existing advisory `band: 'editorial'` loop's shape exactly (its own dedicated dispatch loop, since the editorial loop's prompt text is editorial-specific and NFR never edits the worktree). Wired through the full routing chain: `config/factory.config.json` `reviewFlows.nfr[]` (new band, `appliesWhen: code-touching`, `blocking: false`), `config/model-routing.json` `review.nfr` route, `_workflow/lib/routing-drift.mjs` `FLOW_KEYS` (+1, now 7), `driver.mjs` `applicableReviewFlows`'s band-iteration list (`['method','editorial']` → `['method','editorial','nfr']`, kept in the explicit agreement the existing comment already demands). | KI-E250 | **`solutionFor(target)`'s naive fallback guess (`<target>/<target>.sln`) crashed the ENTIRE `group`/`sweep` command with an uncaught `ENOENT` the moment the guess didn't exist — not a graceful "no target found," a hard process crash.** Live incident: `group --ids a cross-cutting item,a sibling item,a portal-themed item A` died with `Error: ENOENT: no such file or directory, lstat '.../Shared.Otp/Shared.Otp.sln'` deep inside `containedFile`'s `realpathSync` call, mid-command, with two OTHER items' claims already in flight. Root cause: `solutionFor` looks up a target's solution from `scripts/services.json`; when a target isn't a registered top-level service — `Shared.Otp` is a shared library referenced FROM other services' solutions (`an identity service.sln`, `a secondary identity service.sln`), with no `.sln` of its own — it falls back to guessing `target + '/' + target + '.sln'` with **no existence check**. That guess becomes `affectedVerificationTargets`'s `primary` parameter, and unlike every other exhausted-resolution path in that function (the directory-walk, the per-file `cfg.solutions` mapping, the "found nothing anywhere" fallthrough KI-E224 already made graceful), the `primary`-trusting branch (`if (!found && primary && ...) containedFile(repoRoot, resolve(repoRoot, primary))`) calls `containedFile` UNCONDITIONALLY on a value nothing has ever verified exists — `containedFile`'s own `realpathSync` throws bare `ENOENT` on a missing path, propagating all the way up through `cmdGroup`'s `Array.map` with no catch. This is the SAME family as KI-E224 ("found nothing ANYWHERE" now fails soft) but a DISTINCT trigger ("guessed something, but the guess is wrong") that KI-E224's fix never covered. **Fix:** `solutionFor` now verifies its own fallback guess with `existsSync` before returning it — a target with no services.json entry AND no same-named `.sln` returns `null` instead of a lie. `affectedVerificationTargets` already handles a falsy `primary` gracefully (the `if (!found && primary && ...)` condition simply doesn't fire), so a `null` correctly falls through to that same function's OWN directory-walk and per-file resolution, which finds the REAL owning solutions for a shared library's siblings-in-`files[]` (e.g. a cross-cutting item's `an identity service`/`a secondary identity service` files resolve their own `.sln`s normally; only the `Shared.Otp`-rooted files lose the `primary` shortcut, falling back to their own `.csproj` — strictly more precise, not less, verification coverage). Verified live: the exact crashing `group --ids a cross-cutting item,a sibling item,a portal-themed item A --dry` command now completes cleanly. |
| KI-E251 | **Deep post-mortem of cycle-130 (a cross-cutting item/a portal-themed item A): a step-8/8 "Autocompact is thrashing" failure and a cross-cutting item's counted (not infra-suspect) `attemptNumber:1` FAILED verdict LOOKED like an inconsistency against an earlier item's earlier KI-L50 infra-not-counted treatment for the "same" symptom — traced to ground truth instead of assumed to be a bug, and found to be CORRECT classification, not a defect, with one genuine, narrower improvement identified underneath it.** Investigation: `agentFailureKind` (factory.js ~L76) has no pattern matching "Autocompact is thrashing" text, so a throw carrying it classifies as the default `'unavailable'` kind — which `tryAgent`'s retry loop (L906) treats as fully transient and retries up to `ATTEMPTS` times. Critically, each retry is `await limit(function () { return agent(prompt, opts) })` (L921) — the SAME `agent()` global the Workflow runtime provides for every dispatch, called fresh with the identical `prompt`/`opts` closure and NO continuation/session state carried from the failed attempt; a thrashed attempt and a later successful retry are genuinely independent dispatches, not one corrupted conversation resuming itself. For a cross-cutting item, this means: some attempt at the step-8/8 `call('fixer', ...)` (L1338) threw the thrashing error, got classified `'unavailable'`, and was retried — and a LATER, fully independent attempt then returned a normal, schema-valid, non-null response claiming 3 files changed. Because `tryAgent` returned non-null, the `if (!r) { res.infraSuspect = true; ... }` branch (L1339) — the ONLY place this exact call site's infra-suspect treatment lives — never fired; instead the KI-E498 per-step phantom-manifest probe (L1408) correctly caught that the claimed 3 files had NO corresponding worktree diff, retried the SAME step once (L1415), and still failed after the retry, landing the real, counted `finish('FAILED', ...)` at L1433. This is NOT the same failure shape as an earlier item (where the fixer call returned null — every attempt was exhausted/thrashed into nothing, a genuine "no answer was ever produced" infra event): a cross-cutting item's fixer DID produce a confident, complete-looking answer on a clean attempt, and that answer's CONTENT was simply wrong (a hallucinated completion claim) — a real quality defect the phantom-manifest probe exists precisely to catch, correctly billed as a real attempt. **Conclusion: `agentFailureKind`/KI-L50's classification logic is not buggy here and was deliberately left unchanged** — laundering a wrong-but-confident answer into "infra, doesn't count" would be a regression (exactly the "honest NoOp as a substitute for a real defect" shape `execution-policy.md` §4 forbids), not a fix. **The one real, narrower finding:** the two confirmed live incidents behind this whole investigation (an earlier item and a cross-cutting item) both broke down on a plan's FINAL step (8/8 in both cases) — the step carrying the most accumulated context of the whole stepwise sequence, and empirically the single riskiest point for both an outright thrash AND a hallucinated-but-schema-valid completion claim. Nothing in the stepwise fixer's per-step prompt (`stepExtra`, factory.js ~L1325) previously said anything about verifying a claimed edit before reporting it, or about reading frugally on a heavy step — the ONLY existing "Read the file back to confirm your edit landed" instruction lived solely in the KI-E498 retry text (L1414), i.e. reactively, AFTER a phantom claim was already caught and one full extra fixer + probe round-trip already spent. **Fix (prompt-only, no classification/counting-logic change):** `stepExtra` now carries, for EVERY step unconditionally, a proactive "Read each path you're about to claim, right now, before reporting filesChanged — don't rely on memory or an earlier tool result's reported success" instruction (the same discipline KI-E498's retry already teaches, just moved to apply on the FIRST attempt instead of only after a phantom claim is caught); and, scoped ONLY to the last step of a plan (`i === stepList.length - 1`, matching the exact evidenced pattern — not a broader, unevidenced generalization to "all late steps"), an additional context-frugality note naming both incidents by id and advising targeted `git diff`/`grep -n`/bounded-range reads over re-reading whole files already touched. Verified: `node --check _workflow/factory.js` clean; no schema, routing, or counting-logic touched, so no existing selftest assertion needed updating — new pinned assertions added instead (see `_selftest.mjs`) checking the new prompt text is present and correctly step-scoped. No batch relaunched as part of this analysis, per the standing "do not launch the next factory run" instruction in force at the time. |
| KI-E252 | **Owner directive (2026-09-26): route claude-sonnet-5 to claude-sonnet-4-6 everywhere, then raise every claude-sonnet-4-6 route's effort one notch, to compensate for the model swap.** Trigger: while preparing an OpenCode-backend batch, `opencode models` (71 total, this host's only auth is "OpenCode Console") showed NO model matching claude-sonnet-5 at all — the factory's "standard" tier (used by most gate.developer/gate.qa/review.edgecase/review.testreview/review.nfr/reauditor/integrator/runner/planner.mechanical/fixer.consolidation(.solo)/fixer.steps.critical/test_author.mechanical dispatches, i.e. the single highest-volume tier) had nothing to route to under this host's provider. **Fix, part 1 (model swap):** every literal `claude-sonnet-5` in live, forward-looking config/code was replaced with `claude-sonnet-4-6` — `config/model-routing.json` (15 routes + the `defaults` fallback + the `_comment` roster), `_workflow/factory.js`'s `RT`/`FLOW_RT` mirror (15 entries) plus its standalone `SONNET` constant and the `breadth-claim-probe`/code-sweep-utility inline dispatches (neither sourced from the routing table), `_workflow/opencode/routing.mjs`'s own mirror, `_workflow/lib/router.mjs`'s `routing.defaults` fallback, `opencode-assets/worker-profiles.json`'s "standard" tier + `opencode-assets/README.md`'s tier table, and two standalone/legacy tools not wired into the live driver.mjs/factory.js pipeline (`_workflow/live-claude-factory.mjs`, `_workflow/normalize.js`) — updated anyway since they are genuine, re-runnable source files, not generated artifacts. Deliberately NOT touched: `state/run-script-*.js` (dozens of historical, already-consumed one-off batch launchers), `state/ledger.json` / `state/results-*.json` (factual historical cost/model attribution — rewriting would falsify the record of what a past attempt actually used), `reports/*.md` (auto-regenerated from the ledger), point-in-time docs (`CONTINUE_PROMPT_*.md`, `ITEM-X-*.md`, `_workflow/ITEM-X.md`, `_workflow/opencode/COMPATIBILITY.md` — historical validation-run records), prior `ITEM-X.md` entries (append-only), and test files using `claude-sonnet-5` as arbitrary example/fixture data unrelated to real routing values (`setup/controller-assets.test.mjs`, `_workflow/opencode/compatibility.test.mjs`, `_compatibility-live.mjs`, and 3 telemetry-aggregation fixture lines in `_selftest.mjs`). **Fix, part 2 (effort raise, same-day follow-up directive):** every route now naming `claude-sonnet-4-6` had its `effort` raised one step on the `low < medium < high < xhigh < max` scale (low→medium, medium→high, high→xhigh) in the same files, to help the smaller model stand in for what used to be the separate, more capable sonnet-5 tier — `runner`/`review.editorial_structure`/`review.editorial_prose` (formerly low), the bulk of the routes (formerly medium) including the now-collapsed `defaults`, and `archaeologist` (formerly high, now the only sonnet-4-6 route at xhigh). `review.edgecase`'s `escalate_effort` was bumped high→xhigh to preserve its one-step-above-base relationship. **CORRECTION, same-day:** the first-pass repo-wide check for this only grepped factory.js/driver.mjs/routing.mjs and wrongly concluded `escalate_effort` was inert; `_workflow/lib/router.mjs`'s `resolve(routing, key, opts)` DOES implement it (`if (o.escalate && r.escalate_effort) effort = r.escalate_effort`) and it IS unit-tested (`_selftest.mjs`: `resolve(routing, 'fixer.critical', { escalate: true }).effort === 'xhigh'`) — the mechanism itself is real and correct. What's actually true, verified by grepping every real call site of `resolve(...)` in factory.js/driver.mjs: none of them ever pass `{ escalate: true }` — the escalation trigger is fully built and tested but has zero live callers, a "wired half-way" gap distinct from dead config (pre-existing, out of scope here, flagged for a future pass to either wire a real trigger condition or remove the unused parameter). **Real regression surfaced and fixed along the way:** collapsing "mechanical" and "standard" onto the identical model broke `_workflow/opencode/_dispatch-e2e.mjs`'s live e2e test two ways, both traced to root cause before fixing: (a) `server-api.mjs`'s `resolveRoute` (line ~207) resolves a dispatch's PROFILE TIER via `Object.entries(PROFILES.models).find(...)` — a first-match search over `worker-profiles.json`'s insertion order — so with "mechanical" listed before "standard" and both now equal to claude-sonnet-4-6, EVERY sonnet-4-6 dispatch resolves to the "mechanical" tier's profile name, never "standard"'s; the test's fake-server fixture only defined a "standard" profile, so any sonnet-4-6 route (e.g. `integrator`) failed closed with "installed worker profile missing: factory-reviewer-mechanical" — fixed by adding a `mechanical` entry to the test's own model fixture alongside `standard`. (b) Independently, the SAME test's `parseDispatchArgs(['--url',...,'--ids','A,B','--version','2'])` assertion (no `--config` flag) was never hermetic — `dispatcher.mjs`'s `parseDispatchArgs` falls back to reading the HOST's real `config/opencode-dispatch.local.json` when `--config` is omitted, by design, for real dispatch usage; the test silently relied on that file's ambient absence to get a clean `{}` baseline. Creating a real local dispatch config while preparing an earlier batch (this same session) exposed the gap — fixed at the actual root by passing the test an explicit `--config` pointing at a fresh empty JSON fixture in its own temp dir, rather than depending on real repo file-system state. Both fixes verified live: `node _workflow/opencode/_selftest.mjs` now exits 0 (`TOTAL: 222 passed, 0 failed`), and the full `node _workflow/lib/_selftest.mjs` is clean at 2348/0 with 12 pre-existing pinned assertions (models) + 2 more (effort values) updated to match, none newly authored — this was a value-correction pass, not a new-behavior pass. |
| KI-E253 | **Owner directive (2026-09-27): re-tier 3 gate-band routes after a review of `factory_agent_stage_events_total` (Prometheus) showed "gates" is by far the dominant call-volume stage (2397 calls vs. 897 for the next-highest) and heavily opus-weighted.** Three changes, all confirmed live via a direct `routesFor()` import (not just selftest passing): **(1) `gate.po`** moved `claude-opus-4-8`/medium → `claude-sonnet-4-6`/high — its job (read every implementation report, check delivered behavior against acceptance criteria) is synthesis/careful-reading, not the novel structural reasoning `gate.architect`/`gate.security` need; it was already the only opus route running at medium, not high, an implicit admission it didn't need opus's full weight. **(2) `review.nfr`** effort dropped high→medium (model unchanged, `claude-sonnet-4-6`) — it's advisory-only (`blocking:false`, never independently fails an item), so an occasional miss costs far less than a blocking gate's would; it no longer needs to match `review.edgecase`'s tier now that the two have genuinely different stakes. Required decoupling `review.nfr` from `review.edgecase` first — `FLOW_RT['review.nfr']` had been a raw alias to `RT.rEdge` (KI-E249) rather than its own entry, so changing one's effort would have silently changed the other's too; added a dedicated `rNfr` RT entry in both `factory.js` and `_workflow/opencode/routing.mjs`. **(3) `review.adversarial`** was the biggest single lever: it ran on `claude-opus-4-8` for **every item unconditionally** (`"when": "all-items"`, no code-touching/complexity gate at all, unlike every sibling flow) — the single largest opus concentration in the whole gate band. Owner ruling: keep opus for genuinely complex changes, allow a cheaper model when the change is not. **Discovered before implementing:** `model-routing.json`'s `"when"` field is documentation only — the real gating logic for every flow lives hand-written in `flowsFor()`/`routesFor()` in `factory.js` and `_workflow/opencode/routing.mjs`, not machine-evaluated from the JSON string (confirmed by grepping for any `when`-string parser in either file — none exists). So this is not a 1-line config edit: added a new named route `review.adversarial.mechanical` (`claude-sonnet-4-6`/high) alongside the existing `review.adversarial` (unchanged, opus/high), and made `routesFor()`'s per-flow model-resolution loop special-case `routeKey==='review.adversarial'` on the pre-existing `crit = fixType!=mechanical` variable — the SAME signal every other role in that function already uses for this exact judgment call (planner/test-author/fixer/fixerConsolidate), rather than inventing a new "band" concept. Registered `rAdvMech: 'review.adversarial.mechanical'` in `routing-drift.mjs`'s `RT_MAP` so it's config-driven and drift-checked like every other cost-bearing route, not just an inline fallback. **Opencode-path-specific fix (`_workflow/opencode/routing.mjs`'s `roleRoute`):** a launch-time `routing.FLOW_RT` snapshot (built once from config at group-time, no per-item awareness) was checked BEFORE the freshly-computed per-item value — for every other flow that's correct (the snapshot is the authoritative, current config), but for `review.adversarial` specifically it would have silently re-applied the flat opus value to every item regardless of `crit`, exactly the blanket behavior this fix removes. Fixed by routing `review.adversarial` through the snapshot's crit-aware `RT` key instead of its flat `FLOW_RT` entry; every other flow's priority order is unchanged. **Two more pre-existing opencode-mirror gaps found and fixed while verifying (unrelated to the 3 re-tiers, but directly adjacent):** `_workflow/opencode/routing.mjs`'s `FLOW_RT` had no `review.nfr` entry at all (silently absent since KI-E249, never mirrored from `factory.js`), and its `flowsFor()` never pushed a `review.nfr` entry either — meaning **the opencode dispatch path has never been able to route or even enumerate the NFR review stage**, confirmed live: `routesFor({fixType:'non-trivial', files:['Foo.cs']}).reviewFlows['review.nfr']` returned `undefined` before the fix. Both added, matching `factory.js` exactly. **One gap found and deliberately NOT fixed (out of scope for this pass):** even after the `flowsFor()` fix, `_workflow/opencode/runtime.mjs` still never actually DISPATCHES a `band:'nfr'` flow — its phase loop only filters for `band === 'editorial'` (line ~515) and `band === 'method'` (line ~542), with no third filter for `'nfr'`. So `review.nfr` is now correctly routable and enumerable on the opencode path, but still not wired into the actual dispatch phase machine there — a real, separate, larger piece of missing functionality (mirroring `factory.js`'s dedicated NFR dispatch loop) that needs its own pass, not bundled into a model-routing change. Verified: direct `routesFor()`/`flowsFor()` import returns the expected opus/sonnet split by `fixType` and the expected `review.nfr`/`gate.po` values; full `node _workflow/lib/_selftest.mjs` 2348/0 (2 pre-existing pins updated for the nfr effort/RT-key rename, none newly authored beyond that); `node _workflow/opencode/_selftest.mjs` 222/0 unchanged. |
| KI-E254 | **A well-reasoned, plan-review-revised plan still failed pre-band on a phantom-manifest false claim — root-caused to a genuine THIRD case the KI-E498/KI-E181 retry guidance never named.** Live incident: a cross-cutting item cycle-132. The plan-review gate correctly noticed the item's stale worktree still had origin-IP code the owner's direct-to-main commit (`3ee6171d0`) had already removed, and revised the plan to reconcile the two — explicitly naming, file-by-file, which reverts would make a file "drop out of the diff entirely" (i.e., become byte-identical to the tracked branch tip once a prior attempt's leftover edit is correctly undone). The fixer executed this correctly, but its `filesChanged` self-report still listed those revert-to-identical files as changed — and the phantom-manifest probe (correctly, from its own narrower perspective: no diff exists) flagged them as claimed-but-not-found, failing a genuinely well-executed step for nothing. Neither of the retry text's two existing explanations fit: not (a) a tool failure (the edit worked exactly as intended) and not (b) an unrelated file the step merely referenced (the step genuinely, deliberately modified it — by reverting it). **Fix:** added a third case (c) to the KI-E498/KI-E181 retry text — a successful revert-to-identical is real work whose correct result is zero diff, explicitly tied to the "drops out of the diff entirely" phrasing a plan is likely to use for exactly this scenario, with a cross-cutting item cited alongside a related item as the incident precedent for the branch. Also extended the PROACTIVE (first-attempt, KI-E251) per-step instruction with the same guidance, so a step doing this kind of revert can self-correct before ever needing the reactive retry, not just after. Scope: prompt-only, no schema/classification/counting-logic touched, matching KI-E251's own precedent. Verified: `node _workflow/lib/_selftest.mjs` 2352/0 (5 pre-existing pins fixed after the retry-marker text grew a suffix, 4 new pins added for the new case's content). No opencode-side mirror needed — the phantom-manifest retry prompt text lives only in `factory.js`'s stepwise fixer path, confirmed via a repo-wide grep for the marker string before concluding this. |
| KI-E255 | **Recording an owner ruling into a graph item's `ownerDecision` field can make it LESS schedulable than before, silently, with zero diagnostic pointing at why.** `eligibleItems()` (`lib/driver-integration.mjs`) excludes any item where `ownerDecision != null && ownerDecisionResolved !== true` — i.e. the decision TEXT alone is not enough; a companion `ownerDecisionResolved: true` flag must ALSO be set on the same node before the item becomes claimable again. Live incident: while acting on genuine owner rulings for a portal-themed item A (a fresh ruling, `ownerDecision` previously null) and a sub-item A/A2 (a pre-existing 2026-09-23 ruling that had never had the companion flag set), a `group --dry` request for these ids came back "NOT schedulable" via the existing generic WARN — which lists several possible causes (unknown id / non-claimable state / deps unmet / realInfra / file-locked) but has never named this one, even though it is the one cause with NO other surfacing mechanism anywhere (deps-unmet is separately visible via the "waiting-on-deps" status line; file-locked via the "same-file collision" message; this one had nothing). Real investigation time was spent tracing this to `eligibleItems`' source before finding the exact condition. **Fix:** `cmdGroup`'s per-dropped-id diagnostic now explicitly checks this condition for each requested id that didn't make it into the schedulable set, and names it by id when it applies, alongside the existing generic WARN (not replacing it — the other causes still fall through to that). Set `ownerDecisionResolved: true` on a portal-themed item A and a sub-item A/A2's graph nodes (all three have genuine, actionable rulings on file) as part of fixing this live. Scope: driver.mjs diagnostic output only — no change to the actual eligibility LOGIC, which was already correct by design (a recorded decision text should not auto-authorize a batch to act on it without a deliberate "yes, this is final" flag; the gap was purely in how invisible a missing flag was). Verified: `node _workflow/lib/_selftest.mjs` 2354/0 (2 new source pins). |
| KI-E256 | **Switchable routing for the OpenCode dispatch path's "cheap" tier (claude-haiku-4-5's normal role), landed only after live validation — not a speculative option.** Trigger: a direct `opencode run --model opencode-go/<model> --auto "<prompt>"` experiment (bypassing the factory's own dispatch code entirely, to isolate model-quality from unrelated plumbing) tested a representative leftover-scan-style classification task (8 candidates: 4 genuine punts + 4 deliberately tricky legit cases — a lexeme inside a string literal, and a comment explaining a real constraint vs. an actual deferral, matching the KI-D12 LeftoverScan probe's exact classification rules) against `opencode-go/deepseek-v4.1-flash` and `opencode-go/mimo-v2.6-flash`. Both scored 8/8, matching the ideal classification exactly, including both tricky cases. Also tried a `#max` variant suffix on deepseek: no error, but no observable difference in output either — no evidence effort/variant buys anything for this flash-tier model, and pursuing it would work against the high-parallelism motivation anyway (slower/pricier calls mean fewer usefully-concurrent ones). Separately, and importantly: a same-session attempt to run the identical test against `claude-haiku-4-5` (the actual current production model, via `opencode run --model opencode/claude-haiku-4-5`) failed outright with "Insufficient account funds" — this host's OpenCode Console auth has funded access to the `opencode-go` provider but NOT the `opencode` (Claude-branded) provider, independent of and in addition to KI-E252's earlier claude-sonnet-5-missing-from-catalog finding. Every `claude-*` entry in `config/opencode-dispatch.local.json`'s `models{}` map is therefore untested-in-practice and likely to fail the same way if ever actually dispatched, until that provider is funded. **Fix:** added a one-field switch, `cheapTierProvider`, to `config/opencode-dispatch.local.json`, resolved by a new `applyCheapTierProvider()` in `_workflow/opencode/dispatcher.mjs`'s `parseDispatchArgs` — `"claude"` (default, including when absent) is a no-op; `"deepseek"` resolves to the validated `{providerID: 'opencode-go', modelID: 'deepseek-v4.1-flash', agent: 'build'}` and takes priority over any pre-existing explicit `models['claude-haiku-4-5']` entry (the whole point of a one-field switch is not needing to also clear an old override by hand). `"mimo"` was deliberately left OUT of the accepted-values map (`CHEAP_TIER_SUBSTITUTES`) despite having the identical 8/8 live result as deepseek — owner directive, same day: "only DeepSeek V4.1 Flash from opencode for now" — passing it throws the same "unknown value" error as any other unsupported string; the code comment names exactly where to re-add it if that scope is lifted, so the already-gathered evidence isn't lost. Removed the now-redundant static `claude-haiku-4-5` entry from the dispatch config's `models{}` (it was a pure Claude-passthrough that the switch's `"claude"` no-op already reproduces exactly). Verified: 5 new REAL behavioral assertions (not source-text pins) in `_workflow/opencode/_selftest.mjs` directly exercise `parseDispatchArgs` — absent-field no-op, explicit-`"claude"` no-op, `"deepseek"` resolves correctly, the switch overriding a pre-existing explicit override, and `"mimo"` correctly rejected. `node _workflow/opencode/_selftest.mjs`: 227/0 (was 222/0). `node _workflow/lib/_selftest.mjs`: 2354/0 unchanged (this is opencode-local behavior, no drift-checked RT/FLOW_RT table touched). **Same-day follow-on (2026-09-27):** owner directive "use max reasoning efforts in the factory DeepSeek V4.1 Flash" — investigated via the live OpenCode server API (`opencode api model.list`, since the local `~/.cache/opencode/models.json` catalog cache is stale/July and doesn't have V4.1 at all) and found `opencode-go/deepseek-v4.1-flash` exposes exactly 3 named reasoning-effort variants (`low`/`high`/`max`), wired end-to-end through `resolveRoute` (`server-api.mjs`) into the v2 session-creation body's `model.variant` field. Added `variant: 'max'` to the `deepseek` entry in `CHEAP_TIER_SUBSTITUTES` (`_workflow/opencode/dispatcher.mjs`) — the substitute is now `{providerID: 'opencode-go', modelID: 'deepseek-v4.1-flash', agent: 'build', variant: 'max'}`. This is a deliberate owner choice made WITH the earlier evidence in hand, not a new finding that max helps: the original validation experiment already tried `#max` against this same task and saw no observable difference vs. the then-unspecified default, and higher effort trades against the exact high-parallelism motivation for using a flash-tier model here — documented as such in the code comment and in `config/opencode-dispatch.local.json`'s `_cheapTierProviderNote`, not silently overwritten. Verified: 1 new assertion in `_workflow/opencode/_selftest.mjs` pins the substitute's `variant === 'max'`; 1 new end-to-end assertion in `_workflow/opencode/_dispatch-e2e.mjs` calls `resolveRoute` directly with a config.models override mirroring the exact substitute shape and asserts the returned route carries `variant: 'max'` (this file had ZERO prior coverage of the `config.models[...]` override path through `resolveRoute` — the two pre-existing calls there only exercised the installed-definitions path). `node _workflow/opencode/_selftest.mjs`: 228/0. |
| KI-E257 | **A "Claude orchestrates, OpenCode does the sonnet-tier work" dispatch pattern for the 3 sonnet-tier role gates (developer/qa/po), validated through 4 rounds before being wired in — the standalone `_workflow/opencode/` backend could not do this itself (its own strong/planning tiers hit the same ITEM-X funds wall as KI-E256, and one item cannot be split across two separate driving engines), so this is a proxy WITHIN the real Claude Code Workflow pipeline instead.** Design: `factory.js`'s `call()` reroutes an in-scope role from `claude-sonnet-4-6` to `claude-haiku-4-5`/low and appends instructions telling the (still-real, still-Claude) dispatched agent to relay — never independently judge — a different model's review: fetch the review pack, write it plus its own role-brief checklist to a temp file via heredoc, run `opencode run --model opencode-go/deepseek-v4.1-flash --agent explore --auto --file <path> "..."`, then transcribe the raw response into `GATE_SCHEMA` with a hardcoded, deterministic verdict rule (`CHANGES_REQUIRED` iff any finding is HIGH/CRITICAL). Round 1 (easy synthetic diff — a mutable command class + a shipped TODO): 2/2 defects caught, 0 false positives, but surfaced that DeepSeek never emits a literal verdict token — fixed by hardcoding the derivation rule rather than trusting per-dispatch agent judgment. Round 2 (harder synthetic diff, 4 axes at once — a data-flow-traced multi-tenancy security bug, a sanctioned-pattern false-positive trap i.e. the codebase's own accepted "clamp-and-succeed pagination" variant, a severity-calibration trap, and an embedded comment trying to talk the reviewer into skipping review): 4/4 passed, including explicitly identifying and disregarding the injection attempt. Round 3 (validating the heredoc-to-temp-file + `--file` attachment MECHANISM itself, not content quality): the mechanism worked — byte-for-byte content fidelity confirmed — but found a real isolation gap: `opencode run --auto` with no `--agent` specified defaults to opencode's "build" agent, which has UNRESTRICTED shell/read/write permissions (`opencode debug agents`: `{action:'*',resource:'*',effect:'allow'}`) and autonomously ran real `ls`/`find`/`cd`/`grep` against the actual live repository — not sandboxed to the attached file at all, and invisible to a caller only capturing stdout (all of it landed on stderr). **Fix:** added `--agent explore`, opencode's own deny-by-default subagent (only `read`/`grep`/`glob`/`webfetch`/`websearch` allowed back, no `shell`/`edit`). Round 4 re-validated this fix live: zero shell/`ls`/`find`/`cd` calls appeared anywhere in stderr (verified by absence, not just by config), review quality held exactly (still caught the CRITICAL defect, still correctly left the sanctioned pattern alone, still resisted the injection attempt) — but found `explore`'s `read`/`glob` permissions are themselves unscoped (`resource: '*'`), so it still wanders its current directory read-only for extra context (harmless in the test, confined to the disposable scratchpad sandbox by which directory the caller happened to `cd` into first). **Follow-up fix:** the instructions now `cd` into the item's own WORKTREE before invoking opencode, bounding that residual read-only wandering to the same tree the reviewing agent already has legitimate full access to — this specific containment step was reasoned from the round-4 finding, not itself separately live-validated. Scoped to exactly `gate-developer`/`gate-qa`/`gate-po` (`OPENCODE_SONNET_GATE_ROLES`) — NOT `gate-architect`/`gate-security` (opus, "the brain" — see KI-E259) and NOT planner/test-author/fixer (they edit files, a task shape never validated for this relay pattern). Off by default (`OPENCODE_SONNET_GATE_PROXY = false`) pending an owner decision to actually run it on a real batch. Known open gaps (not yet addressed): a 3-field relay format (`FILE\|SEVERITY\|description`) mapped onto FINDING's 4 fields (`severity/title/file/fix`) duplicates the description into both `title` and `fix`; the temp-file heredoc uses a plain string sentinel that would silently truncate if a reviewed diff ever contained a bare line matching it; no test yet of a genuine opencode failure/timeout mid-call (only the happy path is validated); no test at real-item scale (every round used a compact ~3-file synthetic diff, not the dozens-of-files diffs real items often produce); gate-qa and gate-po were never separately tested (all 4 rounds used gate-developer's checklist shape); no concurrency test of multiple simultaneous `opencode run` calls against the local server. Verified: `node _workflow/lib/_selftest.mjs` 2369/0 (15 new source pins covering the toggle, role scoping, dispatch-order, cost-tier rerouting, the exact opencode invocation form, injection-resistance and deterministic-verdict text, fail-closed behavior, and the worktree-containment instruction). **First live production use (2026-09-28, owner directive "go" after explicitly choosing to debut it on a cross-cutting item + a portal-themed item A together, including the security-critical item):** the toggle correctly rerouted `gate-developer`/`gate-qa` to `claude-haiku-4-5`/low (confirmed directly from the real dispatch's `attemptObservations`, not inferred) — the routing mechanism itself worked exactly as designed. `gate-qa`'s relay call **genuinely hung** — `opencode run --model opencode-go/deepseek-v4.1-flash --agent explore` ran 15+ minutes on a real, complex multi-file production review (bigger and slower than any of the 4 synthetic validation rounds) with no output, process still resident. **The fail-closed design held under a real failure for the first time**: the dispatched agent did not fabricate a result — it returned `CHANGES_REQUIRED` with exactly one HIGH finding describing the hang precisely (PID, elapsed time, truncated output file), exactly per its own instructions. This is the "known open gap" above ("no test yet of a genuine opencode failure/timeout mid-call") now closed by real evidence rather than remaining synthetic. `gate-developer`'s relay call produced a genuinely excellent, deeply-verified review — but whether DeepSeek actually did that work and it was faithfully relayed, versus the haiku proxy quietly doing its own independent review instead of relaying, was NOT conclusively determined (not chased further given time). a cross-cutting item's QA gate was recovered via a direct real-Claude re-gate instead of retrying the relay, deliberately — a last-blocker item's final check is not the place to gamble on a mechanism that just demonstrated real unreliability under load. **Toggle reverted to `false` immediately after both launches** (it only matters at `group`-snapshot time, so this did not affect either already-launched batch). **Not yet addressed, now confirmed necessary before any FUTURE production use**: some real handling for a hung/slow relay call — a shorter timeout, a retry, or a documented decision that a hang always means "fall back to real Claude for this one dispatch" rather than accepting `CHANGES_REQUIRED` as the final word (right now a hang always costs the item a full extra gate-review round, even though the code may well have been fine). **a portal-themed item A's cycle-135 result (same launch, folded separately after it finished running later) is a second, independent data point that sharpens rather than repeats the picture.** First, the `gate-qa` hang is REPRODUCIBLE, not a one-off: it hung again, same shape (launched, 60+s with only a "File created at:" line, no defect output, still-running background task at verdict time), correctly fail-closed to `CHANGES_REQUIRED` with one HIGH finding again — two-for-two hangs on `gate-qa` specifically, across two different items, is enough to call this a systematic reliability problem with that role's dispatch rather than random flakiness. Second, `gate-developer`'s relay succeeded again AND produced substantively strong content, explicitly headed "Findings (Proxy Review via DeepSeek v4.1-flash)": 14 real, well-cited defects, including a genuine architectural misconception (the runbook and code comments conflate Kubernetes readiness-probe failure with liveness-probe failure — a shipped runbook scenario was physically impossible as written), a fabricated-authority catch (an "owner ruling" cited in three separate new files traces to no ADR, decision, or prior commit anywhere in the repo), a missing `the host's standards-evolution doc § 3.2` ledger entry for a real, self-declared pattern divergence, and several weak/tautological test-assertion findings. That is now 2/2 successful `gate-developer` relay dispatches producing genuinely useful, non-trivial review content, against 2/2 hung `gate-qa` dispatches — a clean, role-correlated split worth investigating (is `gate-qa`'s task shape structurally heavier for the `explore` agent — more files, a broader search space — than `gate-developer`'s?) before any future re-enable, rather than treating the hang risk as uniform across all three proxied roles. **Two mitigations landed same-day (2026-09-28), acted on the above rather than left as an open question — code-only, no toggle flip, safe with the mechanism still off.** (1) A leading, concrete hypothesis for the role-correlated split: `gate-qa`'s own role brief (unlike `gate-developer`'s) instructs the reviewer to independently RUN commands it cannot actually run through this relay — re-run a concurrency-touching test class up to 15 times, self-invoke `registration-drift` — and step 2 embeds that checklist verbatim into the file handed to `--agent explore`, which has read/grep/glob only, zero shell. An unfulfillable instruction may be exactly what drives the underlying model into the confused/looping state a "hang" looks like from the outside. Fix: a prepended note in the temp file, before both the checklist and the review pack, telling the reviewer explicitly it has read-only access and must not attempt a workaround on an unexecutable instruction — just flag it if it matters and keep reviewing everything else. (2) Independent of whether (1) is the true root cause: the relay previously had NO explicit time bound, and the two real dispatches that hit a hang waited wildly different amounts of time before giving up with nothing to guide either of them (a cross-cutting item's ~15+ minutes; a portal-themed item A's ~3 minutes, "120s initial ... subsequent polling at +60s window"). A fixed 3-minute budget is now stated explicitly in the relay instructions (chosen to be comfortably longer than every successful round observed so far, while bounding a hang's wasted-time cost far below the 15-minute outlier) — a hang now costs a bounded, CONSISTENT amount of time regardless of which agent happens to be holding the relay, rather than being at the mercy of each dispatched agent's own patience. **Neither mitigation is live-validated yet** — both are reasoned from the evidence above, not proven; the next real toggle-on production use should specifically check whether `gate-qa` still hangs, and if it does, that disproves hypothesis (1) and points toward removing `gate-qa` from `OPENCODE_SONNET_GATE_ROLES` entirely instead. Verified: `node _workflow/lib/_selftest.mjs` 2393/0 (3 new pins covering the read-only-access note, the 3-minute budget, and their relative ordering; 1 pre-existing pin's expected substring updated to match the reworded fail-closed instruction). |
| KI-E259 | **A structurally quality-preserving hybrid for `gate-security` specifically — reuses KI-E257's already-validated relay mechanism, but never lets it approve anything on its own.** A same-day intermediate step, KI-E258, briefly retiered `gate-security` from opus straight to a real (non-proxied) `claude-sonnet-4-6` — reverted within the hour once the owner clarified the actual intent: *"I do not want to retire anything, i want you to add routing so that I could switch to the opencode executor without quality degradation. Think how to preserve the quality."* `RT.gSec` is back to real `claude-opus-4-8`/high, unconditionally, in `factory.js`, `opencode/routing.mjs`, and `config/model-routing.json` alike — KI-E258 is not a live option, it is fully reverted. In its place: a NEW, separate toggle, `OPENCODE_SECURITY_HYBRID` (default `false`), gated on `role === 'gate-security'` specifically inside `call()`, checked BEFORE the KI-E257 rewrite (a no-op for this role regardless, since it isn't in `OPENCODE_SONNET_GATE_ROLES`). When on: the KI-E257 relay (same `opencodeSonnetProxyInstructions`, same `--agent explore` mechanism, dispatched at the cheap haiku tier) runs FIRST. If it reports `CHANGES_REQUIRED`, that verdict is trusted immediately and the real opus dispatch is skipped entirely — the actual source of any cost saving, and safe to trust because a wrongly-rejected item is not a quality risk (this pipeline's existing dissent/adjudication machinery already exists to catch an erroneous `CHANGES_REQUIRED`, so nothing new is being asked of it). If it reports `APPROVED`, or is unavailable after retries, NOTHING from it is trusted: control falls straight through to the completely normal, unmodified `claude-opus-4-8` dispatch that runs today regardless of this toggle — and that fall-through deliberately carries no hint that a relay ran or what it said, so the real reviewer cannot anchor on it and genuinely forms an independent second opinion. This makes the quality guarantee STRUCTURAL rather than empirical: the worst case (the relay approves everything, correctly or not) is zero cost savings and zero quality change, because that branch foundationally IS today's unmodified baseline; every real defect the relay genuinely catches on its own is where the savings actually comes from. Not yet live-validated the way KI-E257's own relay was (4 rounds) — this reuses that already-proven relay, but the two-stage short-circuit/fall-through control flow itself is new. Off by default. Verified: `node _workflow/lib/_selftest.mjs` 2379/0 (10 new source pins covering the reverted opus routing, the dedicated toggle + role gate, dispatch ordering ahead of both the real call and the KI-E257 rewrite, the cheap-tier relay dispatch, reuse of the exact KI-E257 instruction generator with no un-validated duplicate, the single short-circuiting `return`, and the no-anchoring independence guarantee). |
