To distinguish "wrong cluster/context" from "genuinely not deployed yet" without guessing, the not-found path now also emits an inventory of every namespace + any same-named deployment anywhere in the cluster. |
| KI-E321 | **An earlier "service not found" turned out to be transient (not yet deployed), not a wrong-cluster problem — confirmed once the service was actually deployed and the full build→load→rollout→health-check→rollback cycle passed end-to-end.** A default-kubeconfig probe added as a defensive diagnostic (emitted only on a not-found) stays as defense-in-depth for a genuine wrong-context case, even though it wasn't the actual cause here. |
| KI-E322 | **Extended the live-deploy harness from a bare health check to real functional API testing, under a hard-gated test-only auth bypass.** A new auth handler accepts a simple test-token header format under every existing auth scheme name, but FAILS CLOSED — it throws at startup if the environment is Production, and is wired only under a dedicated test environment name, never in a committed production manifest. A companion script flips the pod to that environment, port-forwards, runs the item's own HTTP assertions (unauthenticated → 401, impersonation → 403, legitimate owner → reaches the handler), then ALWAYS reverts the pod to its production environment setting via a trap, so the bypass can never persist. |
| KI-E323 | **First functional live-API run failed both authenticated checks with 401 — because the built image only contained the fix branch, not the auth-bypass harness itself (which lived on the main branch).** Fix: the deploy-test now merges the main branch into the fix worktree before building, so the built image reflects what will actually ship once the fix merges (main's test harness + the fix), with a branch-only fallback (functional test degrades, health smoke still runs) on a merge conflict. Verified end-to-end: 3/3 functional checks passed against a real merged build on a live cluster. |
| KI-E324 | **A scheduled/nightly orchestrator run failed immediately with "command not found" for the Node runtime — a missing Node-setup step in that one CI workflow file, present in the factory's other two workflow files but accidentally omitted from this one.** Fix: added the missing Node-setup step, mirroring the other workflows. A red herring in the log (a line that looked like an owner-placed "stop requested" marker) was traced to the failing step's own script output under shell trace mode, not an actual pause file. |
| KI-E325 | **The orchestrator run exited with a hard failure code whenever a single dispatch lane merely timed out / was incomplete — a normal, recoverable state — aborting the whole run and mislabeling resumable items as outright failures.** A lane that timed out returned a generic "lane incomplete" error, and the run loop treated ANY lane error as fatal. Fix: only a small, genuinely-fatal set of error classes (the scheduler itself breaking, or a dispatch that couldn't even launch) now exits nonzero; a timed-out/incomplete lane logs non-fatally, its finished items fold normally, and the unfinished ones simply resume on the next run — the run's own exit code now reflects orchestrator health, not whether every item individually closed. |
| KI-E326 | **A shared, resource-constrained CI runner let multiple workflow runs and an inefficient N-way test matrix pile up and starve each other.** Fix: (1) added a concurrency group with cancel-in-progress to every CI workflow, so a new push cancels its own superseded run instead of queuing behind it; (2) consolidated a large per-project audit matrix into one looped job, since on a single serial runner the "parallel" matrix was actually serial anyway at N× the fixed per-job setup cost. |
| KI-E327 | **A day-1, telemetry-driven self-improvement pass: added a "pre-flight" section to the fixer's role brief listing the specific things the dominant rejecting gate most often fails on, so the fixer produces them in the FIRST pass instead of needing a re-fix round** (the main token-saving lever); enabled build-layer caching for the live-deploy-test harness; added logic to stop auto-retrying an item whose failure REASON changed between attempts (an unstable/under-specified item no longer burns hundreds of thousands of tokens on repeated pointless retries); and started posting the parked-decisions backlog to a tracking issue each run instead of leaving it as a silent file nobody reads. Deliberately did NOT churn the existing, already-tuned model-routing config — the cost win came from fewer wasted attempts, not blind re-routing. |
| KI-E328 | **Moving CI onto a self-hosted runner surfaced three environment-specific failures that looked like code regressions but weren't (these jobs had simply never run in this environment before): (1) a fixed host-port service-container collision, (2) a missing OS utility the hosted runner image ships but this one doesn't, (3) file-watcher-driven inotify-instance exhaustion from several concurrent test processes each registering a config-reload watcher.** Fixes: dynamic ports instead of fixed ones; a portable binary-format check instead of the missing utility; a polling file watcher (which uses no inotify) instead of the default. |
| KI-E329 | **Several container-heavy CI jobs launching test-database containers concurrently on one self-hosted runner overwhelmed its container-daemon and file-descriptor budget.** A shared concurrency group would have serialized them but silently dropped overlapping runs instead of queuing them (the platform keeps only one pending run per group). Fix: labeled one specific runner and routed all the container-heavy jobs to it specifically, so they queue in order on that one runner — one container launch at a time, nothing dropped — while other runners stay free for everything else. Accepted trade-off: those suites now run serially instead of in parallel. |
| KI-E330 | **A security-audit CI step was reporting red on every run, but not because of a real vulnerability — it was the step's own shell semantics.** The underlying audit command can return a nonzero exit code even when a project is completely clean (one project type in the audited set is known to behave this way), and the shell's default fail-fast mode killed the step at the very first project checked, before anything downstream was even audited. The step already correctly parsed the command's OUTPUT for the real positive signal; the fix was to stop letting the unreliable exit code kill the step early (capture output with a non-fatal exit, keep the existing output-based pass/fail check unchanged) rather than disabling the audit. |
| KI-E331 | **A build job was intermittently red with a "child build node exited prematurely" error — resource over-subscription on a shared, multi-tenant CI box, not a compile error.** The build tool spawns one worker process per CPU core by default; with several other things building/running concurrently on the same shared box, the kernel killed worker processes under memory pressure. Fix: capped the per-build worker-process count and disabled cross-invocation worker-process reuse on the affected build steps, trading some build speed for reliability under contention. |
| KI-E332 | **A CI review-tool installation step failed because the runner's package manager had script-execution disabled (a supply-chain hardening setting), silently skipping the post-install step that fetches the tool's actual binary.** Fix: force scripts on for that one specific, trusted, version-pinned install only (leaving the hardening setting in place for every other install), plus a version-check step that fails loudly if the binary still isn't present, instead of silently proceeding with a broken tool. |
| KI-E333 | **A "dispatch by assigning a tracking issue" trigger only ever handled a single item at a time — assigning an issue meant to represent a whole batch of items silently did nothing.** Fix: the trigger now also scans the issue body for an explicit batch directive (an id-list in the same form the orchestrator's own CLI already accepts); when present, it dispatches the full batch through the normal run pipeline instead of looking for a single item id in the issue title. The existing single-item path is untouched. |
| KI-E334 | **A batch dispatch silently no-opped ("no launchable batch") because its items were all in the escalate-oversight tier, which the automatic scheduler deliberately never auto-selects — but the escalate-inclusion override flag that exists for exactly this case was never actually wired from the entry point through to the scheduler, so it was unreachable dead code.** Fix: wired the override flag end-to-end from the dispatch entry point through to the eligibility check. An owner explicitly listing item ids and assigning/dispatching them IS the approval needed to include escalate-tier items for that one dispatch; the default stays auto-tier-only for unattended/scheduled runs. |
| KI-E335 | **A multi-item dispatch lane exceeded the orchestration tool's hard script-size cap and silently did zero work (the launcher refused the oversized script, and the lane just watched for checkpoints that would never appear) — triggered by large per-item specs multiplied across several items in one lane.** Fix: lowered the default max-items-per-lane so a lane carrying several large-spec items stays comfortably under the cap; reversible in one config line, and an adaptive config-tuner may re-adjust it over time. |
| KI-E336 | **Added the ability to task the factory ad-hoc from free text (an issue/ticket) instead of only from pre-scoped backlog items.** A new scoping step turns free text into a properly schema-valid work item with a real file-lock-set and checkable acceptance criteria — a structured `Target:`/`Files:`/`Acceptance:` template parses deterministically; anything else falls back to a best-effort scoping pass that refuses outright (rather than guessing) if it can't find concrete files to lock, and always refuses a request that crosses a documented product-scope boundary. Every ad-hoc task is tagged for owner oversight by design (dispatching it is itself the approval) and the owner sees a scope preview before any code is written. First live run caught two real plumbing bugs in the ingestion step itself (a path-sanitization step that mangled the expected output filename, and the ingested item landing in an array wrapper the parser didn't expect) — both fixed. |
| KI-E337 | **A reporting command crashed outright when a ledger row's note field was an object instead of a string, because the string-formatting call wasn't guarded for that case.** Fix: coerce to string defensively before formatting. Separately observed (not fixed in this pass, flagged for a follow-up): a batch of parked items existed in the live ledger but had dropped out of the current backlog graph entirely, degrading that reporting view's severity/target columns to unknowns — a reconciliation pass between the two is still needed. |
| KI-E342 | **An owner-decision digest report was unusable in practice: entries were hard-truncated at a small character count (silently dropping the second half of a two-option question), structured decision objects rendered as the literal text "[object Object]", and the majority of parked items had no ledger note at all so they showed nothing.** Fix: extract readable text from either a string or object note; fall back through several other sources of context (a dedicated decision write-up file, a queue-tracking file's own framing, the park reason) so every parked item shows SOME real description; parse an explicit "Option A / Option B" structure out of free text into a clean list; derive a severity label from the item id pattern when the backlog graph doesn't have the item; raise the truncation cap substantially and add a dedicated "full decisions" section alongside the compact table. |
| KI-E343 | **A status-dashboard UI had a tab-switch render race: switching views during a slow load let the PREVIOUS view's async render finish late and overwrite the view the user had already switched to.** Fix: a monotonically-increasing render-generation counter captured by each async render after it starts; a render whose captured generation no longer matches the current one is a no-op on completion (success or failure), instead of blindly painting over whatever the user is currently looking at. |
| KI-E344 | **An owner-decision digest still wasn't actually sufficient to decide FROM — a reader had to go find the full write-up elsewhere even after the previous formatting fix.** Rewritten to lead with the FULL per-decision write-up (not a truncated summary table), ranked by severity×age, pulling from the richest available source per item (a dedicated decision file, then a tracking-queue write-up, then whatever structured note exists), with a much higher content cap for genuinely authored write-ups. |
| KI-E345 | **Auto-escalated items (exhausted retry attempts, no human-authored write-up) showed only "needs a human" in the decision digest, with no actual context to decide from.** Fix: synthesize real decision context for these from what the ledger ALREADY records — which review gates passed vs blocked, and the last failure note (which often names the specific remaining defect) — plus a standard set of resolution options (re-run / fix manually / override / close), derived entirely from existing structured data, nothing fabricated. |
| KI-E346 | **A graph-rebuild command wiped the entire backlog graph down to just-ingested items, because it REPLACED the graph from a transient staging directory instead of merging into the existing one — and that staging directory is gitignored, so a fresh checkout has it empty.** Mitigated by restoring the graph from history and appending the just-ingested items; flagged as needing a genuine merge fix (addressed more completely in a later entry) plus a safety check that fails loudly if the backlog item count ever drops below a sane floor. |
| KI-E347 | **An adaptive item-selection algorithm kept picking items the scheduler always rejects, so entire dispatch runs completed with zero items actually launched.** The selection pools read every READY item without excluding the tier that requires explicit owner inclusion — and because never-tried items score artificially high in an explore/exploit bandit, the algorithm consistently ranked the always-rejected tier highest, submitting them, having them correctly rejected, and never falling back to the items that actually were schedulable. Fix: a shared "actually schedulable" filter applied consistently across every selection algorithm (bandit, hill-climbing, simulated-annealing perturbation). |
| KI-E348 | **An explicit owner override to include a normally-oversight-gated tier correctly reached the scheduler, but a downstream pipeline step never received the same signal — so those items still got silently routed to an "awaiting human" terminal state instead of proceeding to completion, even after every review gate had already approved them.** Fix: thread the override flag all the way through to the specific step that was still checking the item's tier in isolation, so an owner's explicit pre-approval is honored consistently at every decision point, not just the entry one. |
| KI-E349 | **A nightly batch-selection algorithm had no preference for items that unblock other blocked work, so dependency chains could stay stalled for a long time even while plenty of dispatch capacity was going to independent, lower-leverage items.** Fix: added a scoring bonus proportional to how many blocked items a candidate would directly unblock, so items that unlock the most downstream work get prioritized; also widened the nightly run's per-batch size since the new scoring made larger batches more clearly worthwhile. |
| KI-E350 | **The orchestration tool's own dispatch script hit a hard, fixed size cap on the script format it uses to launch a batch — the core engine file alone was already close to the limit, so adding even one item's data pushed it over, and an over-cap script is silently refused outright.** Fixed by trimming verbose inline documentation/comments out of the engine file in several passes (moving the detail into the external known-issues log instead, which has no such cap) to claw back enough headroom for real batches to launch. |
| KI-E351 | **The SAME graph-wipe-via-replace bug from an earlier entry recurred a second time, because the earlier mitigation fixed the SYMPTOM (restored the data) but not the actual replace-instead-of-merge logic.** Properly fixed this time: the merge command now loads the EXISTING graph first and genuinely merges into it — new items from staging are appended, existing items are preserved untouched — with an early no-op when staging is empty, instead of ever writing a wholesale replacement. |
| KI-E352 | **An append-only event-log file grew unbounded (tens of megabytes, many tens of thousands of lines) with no rotation, eventually large enough to trigger the hosting platform's large-file warning on every push.** Fix: split the existing file into dated monthly archives, added a rotation helper that moves older-than-current-month lines into a new archive at the start of each run, and made the log-reading code transparently concatenate all archives plus the current file when no specific file is requested. |
| KI-E353 | **One particular review gate was a disproportionate share of fix-attempt rejections, and the fixer's role brief had a "how to pass the OTHER common gate" pre-flight checklist already, but nothing equivalent for THIS gate.** Fix: added the equivalent pre-flight checklist (the specific structural/layering/registration patterns this gate most commonly rejects) to the fixer's role brief, mirroring the existing section for the other gate, so both get addressed in the first pass instead of needing a re-fix round. |
| KI-E354 | **A self-improvement analysis script only read one data source (an optional metrics-server query) for which review gates cause silent extra work, missing gates that cause a re-fix round without ever being the FINAL rejecting gate** (so they never show up in a "final decisive gate" tally even though they cost real extra calls) **, and separately had no way to detect a specific pipeline stage running abnormally long with no alerting on it.** Fix: query the metrics server for both the full rejection-count-by-gate breakdown (not just final/decisive) and per-stage duration averages; add a "silent re-fix bottleneck" proposal type (a gate's total rejection count far exceeds its decisive-rejection count) and a "stage duration anomaly" proposal type (a stage averaging well beyond its typical duration). |
| KI-E355 | **A "select items for the next dispatch" command picked items without immediately marking them as claimed, so a second selection call (or a dispatch that failed before it reached its own claim step) could pick the SAME items again and launch a duplicate run for the same work — burning the cost twice.** Fix: the selection command now transitions each picked item to claimed and persists that immediately, before even emitting the launch script, so no subsequent selection call can re-pick an item that's already spoken for; an existing stale-claim recovery mechanism handles the case where the launch itself never actually starts. |
| KI-E356 | **A test-authoring role brief had no guidance for items whose fix spans multiple independent services/components — a test scoped to just one side of a cross-service interaction can't actually prove the full contract.** Fix: added guidance requiring, for a multi-service item, a caller-side test (asserting the correct request shape is sent) AND a callee-side test (asserting it's handled correctly), with an explicit note in the test author's own output about what only a true end-to-end/live check could additionally cover — and never attempting to spin up multiple full service hosts in one unit-test class, which is out of scope for that role. |
| KI-E357 | **A verification role had no instruction to check ALL of the directories/components a fix actually touched when a change spanned more than one — it only verified the ONE it happened to assume was relevant, missing real build/test failures elsewhere in the same diff.** Fix: added guidance for the verification role to inspect the fix's own "files changed" record for distinct top-level component directories with real code changes, run the full build+test cycle for EACH one found, and explicitly name every verified component in its evidence output (so a later check that greps for named components catches an accidentally-skipped one). |
| KI-E358 | **Some fix attempts failed an entire expensive review-gate round solely over a LOW-severity, purely cosmetic finding — a misleading comment or stale doc-string where the actual logic was already correct — wasting a full re-fix-and-re-gate cycle over what amounts to a few words of text.** Fix: when every rejecting finding across all gates is explicitly flagged low-severity-and-cosmetic (a new field reviewers can set), a lightweight cosmetic-only patch step runs BEFORE escalating the dispute: a bounded fix pass corrects just the comment/text, then only the previously-failed gates re-run; if they all now approve, the item proceeds as if it had passed originally, and if any genuinely still fails (the logic really was wrong), it falls through to the normal process unchanged. |
| KI-E359 | **The factory's self-improvement proposals came only from its own empirical run-history patterns, with no mechanism to incorporate lessons from the broader agentic-AI research literature (self-reflection-on-failure, self-critique-before-committing, branching/comparing multiple reasoning paths, bootstrapping from successful traces, calibrated confidence).** Fix: added a research-informed proposal generator running a half-dozen literature-motivated heuristic checks (e.g. "an item failed repeatedly with no reflection artifact" → propose adding a reflection step; "a gate's rejection rate is high" → propose branching/comparing plan alternatives before committing), queuing a small, deduplicated number of fresh proposals per run. |
| KI-E360 | **The self-improvement analyzer only ever ran as a side effect of an active dispatch — with no dispatch, it made zero progress, including the new research-informed proposal generator.** Fix: confirmed/exercised the analyzer's existing standalone CLI invocation path and wired it (and the research-informed generator) into a periodic scheduled job, addressed together with the next entry. |
| KI-E361 | **The factory's adaptive tuning algorithms and self-improvement analysis only ever executed as a side effect of an active, manually-triggered dispatch — if nobody dispatched anything for a while, none of them made any progress at all, including research-informed proposal generation.** Fix: added periodic scheduled jobs (daily / weekly / twice-weekly cadence) that run the self-improvement analyzer, the research-informed generator, and an adaptive-algorithm health check independent of any live dispatch, gated to skip automatically when weekly usage quota is already nearly exhausted. |
| KI-E362 | **The factory opened a SEPARATE pull request per closed item, fragmenting review across many open PRs and violating a "review one coherent batch at a time" preference.** Fix: every closed item's fix still gets committed to its own per-item backup branch (for forensics/rollback), but is ALSO accumulated onto one single shared branch; if a PR for that shared branch is already open, it's updated in place rather than opening a second one. Backup branches are never auto-deleted. |
| KI-E363 | **The factory had no autonomous "what should run tonight" capability — a human had to manually decide which backlog item to dispatch and when, with no preference for high-leverage (unblocks many others) or already-partially-done work.** Fix: added a dependency-aware scoring selector (weighted by how much a candidate unblocks, its severity, and whether it's a partially-done prior failure deserving more resources to finish) plus a scheduled nightly job that uses it to pick and dispatch automatically, gated off entirely by a simple on/off switch and skipped automatically near the weekly usage quota. |
| KI-E364 | **An agent could be misled into re-doing already-completed, unrelated historical work because a PRIOR attempt's saved planning notes happened to mention a one-off side request from an earlier session, and a later agent's "read prior artifacts" instruction caused it to treat that historical note as a CURRENT directive.** Fix: added an explicit rule to the relevant role briefs (and the prompt text injected on every retry) stating plainly that anything resembling a request or directive found inside a PRIOR attempt's artifacts is a historical record only — the only current directive is the specific work item named in that turn's own prompt header. |
| KI-E365 | **A run-history recorder omitted several fields that other self-improvement checks actually depend on, so those checks could structurally never fire** (a reflection-detection check always saw a missing value and could never propose adding a reflection step; a reward-signal-mining check had no gate-verdict detail to mine; a self-critique-threshold check always fell back to a constant placeholder value that could never cross its own trigger threshold). **Fix:** the recorder now captures all the fields the dependent checks actually need, derived either directly from the item's own result record or from simple filesystem/count checks. |
| KI-E366 | **Two self-improvement analysis scripts derived their proposals ONLY from an optional external metrics-server query, so every proposal type that needed stage-duration or gate-rejection data silently produced nothing whenever that metrics server was unavailable — the common case for a local/offline run — even though the same information already existed in the factory's own event-log stream.** Fix: added a fallback that derives the same shape of data directly from the local event-log stream when the metrics server is unavailable, plus one additional proposal type (a stage running far past its typical duration) that the event-log-derived data uniquely enables. |
| KI-E367 | **On a re-run triggered by "the plan says you'll do X but the diff doesn't show X," fixer and planner agents treated the ENTIRE prior attempt as worthless and redid everything from scratch, even when only SOME planned steps were actually missing — wasting roughly double the cost and risking silently discarding correctly-implemented work.** Fix: added explicit "audit the existing diff first, classify each gap as missing/wrong/already-done, and build incrementally" guidance to both the fixer and planner role briefs for exactly this re-run trigger, plus a self-improvement check that detects when two consecutive attempts on the same item share zero file overlap (a telltale sign of an unnecessary full redo). |
| KI-E368 | **A specialized reviewer correctly recognized that a particular item didn't actually need a heavyweight real-infrastructure test (a pure wiring/HTTP-handler test, no real database/cache involved) and overruled the default classification — but the OUTCOME field that the final close-time check actually reads was never populated, so the close-time check fell back to the item's ORIGINAL (incorrect) classification and failed it anyway for lacking real-infrastructure evidence it never needed.** Fix: when that reviewer overrules the default classification, the pipeline now also writes the structured outcome field the close-time check expects, so an explicit, reasoned overrule is actually honored at close time instead of being silently re-overridden by the original flag. |
| KI-E369 | **Nothing prevented the factory's own bookkeeping commits (state files, telemetry) from landing directly on the main branch while real code fixes accumulated separately on the shared batch branch, creating two diverging histories that then conflicted when the batch branch's PR was eventually merged.** Fix: three layers of the same guard (a script-level check, a local git hook, and a server-side branch-protection rule) all block anything from reaching the main branch except through the one sanctioned batch-PR merge path. |
| KI-E370 | **A maintenance job referenced a command that didn't actually exist yet — the scheduling config called a specific subcommand that had never been wired into the main CLI tool, so that scheduled step always silently failed to do anything.** Fix: implemented the missing subcommand (parses the self-improvement proposal files already being generated into real backlog items, with stable content-derived ids and an owner-oversight tier by default) and wired it into the CLI's dispatch table. |
| KI-E371 | **A detection probe for a particular false-positive-prone defect pattern had no way to express its own uncertainty, so EVERY positive detection — confident or not — hard-failed a fix outright, including borderline cases that were plausibly a benign artifact rather than a real defect.** Fix: added an optional confidence field to that probe's schema; below a threshold, the result now records as approved-with-a-low-confidence-note instead of a hard fail, leaving the final close-time authority to make the real call. |
| KI-E372 | **A plan-quality probe had the same no-confidence-field gap as the previous entry, so an uncertain "plan doesn't actually cover this" verdict could trigger an expensive full plan revision even when the probe itself wasn't sure.** Fix: same pattern as the prior entry — added a confidence field, gated the expensive revision-triggering path behind a minimum-confidence threshold, and recorded a low-confidence note instead of acting on an uncertain signal. |
| KI-E373 | **A disproportionate share of one particular review-gate's rejections traced back to the PLANNING stage choosing a structurally wrong initial approach — and the planner only ever got one shot at framing the approach.** Fix: added an optional "generate two independent candidate approaches, have a cheap judge pick the stronger one, then hand the winning approach's framing to the real planning call as a seed hint" step before the main planning call, for fresh (non-reused) plans. Net cost is a couple of extra cheap calls per fresh plan, cheaper than the gate-rejection-and-refix cycle it's meant to prevent. |
| KI-E374 | **A plan-feasibility probe had the same no-confidence-field gap as two earlier entries, with the same risk (an uncertain "not feasible" verdict triggering a costly, possibly-unnecessary plan revision).** Fix: identical pattern — added a confidence field and a minimum-confidence gate before acting on a feasibility gap, consistent with the same fix applied to the sibling plan-quality probe. |
| KI-E375 | **A cost-snapshot recording call was missing one of its required arguments at its post-fold call site, causing a silent crash in that one function on every single fold** (the fold itself still succeeded first, so the user-visible effect was just a quietly-missing cost snapshot, not a failed fold). **Fix:** supplied the missing argument. |
| KI-E376 | **A meaningful share of fix attempts were rejected by the vacuous-test detector — a shipped test whose only assertion sat behind a conditional early-return that the test environment always took, so the test passed unconditionally regardless of whether the actual fix worked.** Fix: added explicit pre-flight guidance to the fixer's role brief to make sure every assertion is genuinely reachable on all code paths it will execute under, use the sanctioned conditional-skip mechanism for a real environment-dependent skip instead of a silent early return, and verify the test genuinely fails on the pre-fix code before considering it done. |
| KI-E377 | **A pre-band scan step (checking the working diff for leftover debug/stub code) averaged many times longer than its typical duration on large worktrees, with occasional extreme outliers, because the underlying diff/file-listing calls it shells out to had no timeout at all and could hang indefinitely.** Fix: added reasonable timeouts to those calls; a timeout now throws and is caught by the existing best-effort error handling (the scan reports a clean/zero result rather than hanging the whole pipeline). |
| KI-E378 | **Self-improvement proposals were generated on every single fold but never automatically turned into real, dispatchable backlog items — that conversion step existed but was only ever invoked manually.** Fix: added an automatic trigger that runs the ingestion step periodically (after a small number of completed runs, tracked via a simple persistent counter) instead of requiring a human to remember to run it. |
| KI-E379 | **No visibility existed into which individual dispatch batches were consuming the most weekly usage quota — the underlying per-item cost data was already being recorded, just never surfaced anywhere in the orchestrator's own output.** Fix: added a log line after every batch fold summarizing that batch's token consumption alongside week-to-date usage against a configurable weekly budget. |
| KI-E380 | **Failed/escalated items sat for several batches before their failure patterns got analyzed into improvement proposals, because that analysis was tied to the same multi-run threshold as the ingestion trigger above — slowing remediation of preventable, recurring failure classes.** Fix: added a per-batch (not multi-run-delayed) failure-pattern analyzer that reads every failed/escalated item's own failure record, categorizes it by pattern, writes a targeted improvement proposal, and immediately ingests it — bypassing the multi-run delay — deduplicated per failure-category-and-item so the same pattern on the same item doesn't spam duplicate proposals. |
| KI-E381 | **A re-run of a previously-failed item picked up a stale, DIFFERENT machine's absolute file path from an earlier attempt's saved artifacts (a prior run on a different host/checkout left its own absolute path in the failure notes), and while the existing out-of-tree-path safety check correctly caught the mismatch and triggered a recovery retry, the recovery guidance didn't clearly explain HOW to reconstruct the correct path from a foreign one, so the retry still got it wrong.** Fix: the recovery guidance now explicitly detects when a bad path's root doesn't match the current worktree's own root at all (a different host/checkout entirely, not just a relative-vs-absolute mistake) and spells out exactly how to strip the foreign prefix and rebuild the correct path; the same warning was added proactively to every retry's prompt, not just the recovery path. |
| KI-E382 | **Two specific, well-understood failure classes (an agent claiming to have written files that don't actually appear in the diff; an agent call returning nothing after retries, a transient-infrastructure signal) were never automatically reset for another attempt, because the automatic-retry classifier had no rule for either pattern, and a separate "this looks like transient infrastructure" shortcut skipped the classifier entirely on the assumption that a DIFFERENT code path handled infrastructure retries — but that other path only runs during a live dispatch, so an item that failed this way outside that window stayed stuck forever.** Fix: added explicit classifier rules for both patterns, and changed the infrastructure-shortcut path to actually perform the retry-and-reset itself instead of assuming something else would. |
| KI-E383 | **A per-batch failure-analysis dedup mechanism was purely acknowledgement-based: once any pattern had been turned into ONE proposal, it was permanently skipped on every later batch regardless of whether that proposal was ever actually acted on, ingested successfully, or even still existed — so a recurring failure class got exactly one attempt at a fix, and if that attempt itself failed or was never picked up, nothing else ever happened for it.** Fix: redesigned the dedup check to actually look at the OUTCOME of the resulting improvement item (closed → truly done, skip; in-progress or very recently queued → being worked, skip; anything else → act again), with escalating urgency language on repeated unresolved occurrences. |
| KI-E384 | **The escalating-urgency design from the previous entry introduced a delay before demanding real code action — waiting for a pattern to recur two or three times before treating it as serious — which is wrong for failure classes that are cheaply, mechanically fixable on the very first occurrence.** Fix: replaced the recurrence-count delay with a preventability-tier system instead: a pattern with a KNOWN, mechanical one-line fix gets that fix applied directly and immediately on first occurrence; a pattern with a known-but-less-trivial fix gets a high-priority proposal on first occurrence (not after waiting); only genuinely investigation-needed patterns get a normal-priority proposal. Recurrence count still adds useful context but no longer gates whether action happens at all. |
| KI-E385 | **No durable per-run metrics store existed for batch telemetry — close_rate, failure_rate, tokens_per_close, gate verdicts and item-level outcomes were logged to stdout but never persisted queryably, making hill-climbing and long-term optimization impossible.** Fix: added a PostgreSQL-backed metrics store (`factory_runs`, `factory_item_runs`, `factory_hill_state`, `factory_improvement_outcomes` tables, with vector embeddings for similarity search via a small embedding sidecar) wired into the orchestrator's post-batch hooks. A hill-climb routine reads recent runs, computes a fitness score (close_rate weighted against failure_rate and efficiency), and emits lane-count/model-efficiency/exploration suggestions. All calls are non-fatal (try/catch) — telemetry never crashes the factory. |
| KI-E386 | **The code-review and refuter adversarial passes used the most expensive model tier for ALL items regardless of severity/band, consuming premium-model budget on MEDIUM/LOW items where a cheaper model is sufficient.** Fix: downgraded the code-review pass to a mid-tier model globally; added a severity-conditional refuter route (top-tier only for CRITICAL/HIGH, mid-tier otherwise) while keeping the security gate's own independent top-tier audit as the backstop. |
| KI-E387 | **Two quality gaps: test-author agents occasionally wrote assertions that always pass regardless of production state; and items with low-confidence fixes went straight to the full gate band with no pre-gate signal, causing avoidable expensive gate failures.** Fix: added an explicit instruction never to write a tautological assertion; added a post-fixer confidence self-assessment — a fixer rating its own confidence low triggers a cheaper pre-gate adversarial review, failing the item early and cheaply instead of burning the full gate band. |
| KI-E388 | **Fixer agents failed the pre-gate documentation-sync check despite existing guidance, wasting hundreds of thousands of tokens per occurrence. Root cause: (1) the guidance was buried without failure-consequence framing; (2) newly created test files appeared as untracked to git and were invisible to gate reviewers who only check the tracked diff, so reviewers saw "no tests written" and failed the item.** Fix: added an explicit failure-consequence block to the fixer brief with mandatory self-verification commands (diff + untracked-file check) whose output must be recorded, plus guidance that new test methods must land in an existing tracked test class rather than a new untracked file. |
| KI-E389 | **Worktree creation could silently return a path even when neither the primary creation path nor its "already exists" retry actually created the directory — an inner catch-all swallowed every error.** The isolation guard would then fail the item immediately with no code written, with no indication of the real cause. Fix: added an explicit existence check after worktree creation that throws a descriptive error instead of silently trusting an unverified path. |
| KI-E390 | **A documentation-sync pre-gate check failed the item immediately on any gap, forcing a full expensive retry of the entire item for what is usually a small, targeted documentation-only fix.** Fix: on a documentation gap, attempt a narrow, cheap documentation-only auto-amend first; only fail the item if the gap survives the amend. Wrapped in try/catch so any amend-path error degrades gracefully to the original hard-fail behavior. |
| KI-E392 | **Worktree creation failed with "branch already exists" when a prior crashed run left a stale branch with no associated worktree directory — colliding with the fix for the sibling silent-failure bug above.** Fix: changed worktree creation to force-reset the branch to its base regardless of whether it already exists, collapsing the "fresh" and "stale branch" cases into one code path, with an old-git fallback for hosts whose git version lacks the force flag. |
| KI-E393 | **Three accuracy fixes in the same pass: (1) a run-history field recorded null for fully-escalated items (all gates passed, but the item was still escalated), causing the self-improvement analyzer to misclassify them as infra failures; (2) the analyzer's infra-failure count over-counted by including those escalated items; (3) the fixer brief lacked any test-regression-prevention guidance, letting a fixer change a method signature without auditing its callers and silently breaking existing tests.** Fix: corrected the run-history field's priority chain, excluded escalated items from the infra-failure count, and added a "test regression prevention" section requiring a full test-suite run plus a pre-change caller audit before any signature change. |
| KI-E394 | **Mechanical (style/naming-class) fixes consumed as many tokens as complex security changes, because every item unconditionally ran the full adversarial-review flow set and a fixed step budget regardless of how bounded the change was.** Fix: gated the adversarial/edge-case/test-review flows behind a mechanical-item check (skipped for mechanical fixes), reduced the gate panel for mechanical items to a single developer-gate check, lowered the fixer's step budget for mechanical items, and added explicit prompt guidance to apply only basic style/pattern rules rather than the full cross-cutting rule set. Estimated token reduction per mechanical item: ~60-70%. Items in a small set of high-risk themes (security, money-correctness, concurrency, data-consistency) are explicitly excluded from this fast path regardless of fixType. |
| KI-E395 | **Complex items in high-risk themes (security, money-correctness, concurrency, data-consistency) had a 0% first-attempt close rate. Root-cause analysis of several failures found six recurring causes: inventing a new pattern instead of reading an existing sibling; writing cross-service facts from memory instead of reading the actual code; writing test methods with zero real assertions; covering only the happy path and missing a money-correctness edge case; skipping the root-cause-touch check because the changed files were test-only; and changing a method signature without auditing callers.** Fix: broadened the root-cause-touch probe to fire for any non-pure-coverage code change; added a four-part comprehension mandate to the fixer prompt for this item class (read sibling patterns first, read actual code before cross-service claims, enumerate boundary cases before writing money-correctness tests, audit callers before any signature change); added a mandatory assertion-count check before the fixer may proceed past its own red-proof step. |
| KI-E396 | **Three developer-gate accuracy/cost issues: a stale gate verdict persisted across attempts and triggered on an unrelated concern; the gate raised out-of-lane findings (product-acceptance opinions, architecture preferences) that belong to other gates; and routing used the most expensive effort tier even for the narrowed LIGHT-band checklist.** Fix: added a short-circuit that skips loading the full role-persona skill for LIGHT-band items (the narrowed checklist is applied directly); added an explicit "not in this gate's lane" section prohibiting out-of-lane findings, with a note to avoid duplicate findings when a parallel security gate also runs; reduced the LIGHT-band developer-gate's effort tier since its checklist is pattern-matching, not deep reasoning. |
| KI-E397 | **A plan scoped to one endpoint missed that a sibling code path computed and persisted the same value independently — the fix shipped with a correctness residual that many approving reviewers missed and only a late, expensive adversarial pass caught.** Fix: the plan-quality probe now judges a fourth dimension — when a plan changes how a value is computed/validated/persisted, it must require a grep for other call sites that compute/read/write the same value or produce/consume the same event, and account for each one (or explicitly scope it out by name). Catches the class at plan time instead of at the expensive late-review stage. |
| KI-E398 | **The shared per-item prompt prefix (role brief, doc map, guardrails) was re-billed on every agent dispatch with no cache reuse option.** Fix: added an opt-in prompt-cache TTL parameter on every agent dispatch, gated behind a runtime-version check (an unsupported runtime would reject the option on every call) and defaulted off. |
| KI-E399 | **A retry after a dispatch failure repeated the identical prompt, so a failure that needed a correction (not a rerun) burned the whole retry budget without ever changing course.** Fix: on retry after a throw, the prior error is appended to the prompt tail (prefix unchanged, so prompt-cache hits are preserved) with an explicit diagnose-and-avoid instruction. A retry with no surfaced error still repeats the identical prompt, since there is no error information to correct against. |
| KI-E400 | **Two common cheap-failure modes both died at a pre-band probe that re-derived evidence the test-author should have self-verified: a missing red-proof marker on disk, and a red-proof recorded against the wrong test class.** Fix: the test-author brief now mandates a self-check before returning — re-grep the red-proof transcript and confirm both that a nonzero marker exists and that its test class matches what the run command actually targets. Costs one grep instead of a full pipeline retry. |
| KI-E401 | **A deterministic registration-drift lint was re-run independently by up to seven different review touchpoints for the same diff, even though the pre-band probe already ran it once and teed the output.** Fix: the relevant gate/review briefs now read the pre-band probe's teed output first and only re-run the lint themselves if that file is absent or empty; exactly one gate remains the designated independent re-runner as a convergence-proof check. |
| KI-E402 | **Fully-gated mechanical items with an "escalate" autonomy tier parked for human sign-off even when the diff was trivial and fully verified.** Fix: added an opt-in config flag that, when enabled, downgrades a mechanical non-CRITICAL escalate-tier item's autonomy tier to auto at emit time, so a fully-verified tiny diff closes instead of parking. Off by default — this is a deliberate product-policy choice per host. |
| KI-E403 | **The in-process batch-launch channel has a hard size cap; a large comment-heavy engine source file plus its briefs snapshot could exceed it, making a batch unlaunchable.** Fix: added a string/template/regex-literal-aware comment stripper used only for the launch-channel payload (never touching the real source on disk) — comment-internal newlines are preserved so stack-trace line numbers stay identical, and a batch-injection sentinel is protected via a string literal rather than a comment so stripping cannot remove it. Correctness verified via a syntax check on the stripped source plus a parity test proving the raw and stripped sources drive an identical deterministic test lifecycle. |
| KI-E404 | **A separate engine line's band-routing and test-review-skip changes for mechanical items were evaluated and deliberately NOT ported, because they were superseded by this line's own mechanical fast-path and comprehension-mandate fixes (above), which are strictly more aggressive.** Recorded so the decision is not re-litigated: if mechanical-item cost comes up again, extend the existing fast path rather than reintroducing a separate band rule. |
| KI-E405 | **The architect gate rejected a meaningful fraction of complex-item runs on three recurring, cheaply-detectable patterns — spending the full expensive gate band before discovering a layering violation.** The three patterns: a handler saving changes directly instead of through the shared unit-of-work; a core/application file gaining a new reference to an infrastructure/persistence namespace; and an event publish occurring outside its owning transaction scope. Fix: added a cheap pre-band probe that greps for these three patterns on complex (FULL-band) code-change items; on a hit, a bounded fixer amend runs before the gate band, so the diff is clean by the time the expensive review starts. |
| KI-E406 | **A notable fraction of recorded runs showed a null decisive-gate value, misclassifying them as unexplained infra failures, when the actual cause was a pre-band or early-pipeline agent returning null (an infra/credit failure) before any gate had a chance to reject anything.** Fix: when a run fails for exactly this reason, the fold now auto-tags it with a dedicated infra-failure gate verdict, so the failure-analysis histogram counts it as a distinct, correctly-labeled class instead of lumping it into "no gate verdict". |
| KI-E407 | **The vacuous-test scanner's assertion-detection pattern required an assertion method name to be followed immediately by an opening parenthesis, so a validation-library's family of differently-named assertion helpers (which share the same prefix but have additional trailing characters before the parenthesis) were misdetected as having no real assertions.** Fix: broadened the detection pattern to match the shared prefix followed by any word characters before the parenthesis, recognizing both the base assertion form and validation-library helper variants. |
| KI-E408 | **A post-amend re-verification step only re-ran the specific test class an amend targeted, not the full suite — letting a regression in a different, untouched test class reach the expensive gate band undetected, which then had to discover and report it at full gate cost.** Fix: extended the post-amend verification to also run the full suite after the targeted filter, and gate on an explicit suite-pass signal (missing marker treated permissively for backward compatibility, but an explicit suite failure blocks). |
| KI-E409 | **A test-class-name mismatch between what the test run command targets and what actually exists in the worktree was only caught at fold time, after the entire expensive gate band had already run.** Fix: added a cheap probe immediately after the red-proof is confirmed (before any fix or gate work) that extracts the targeted class name and greps the worktree to confirm it exists, failing immediately and cheaply if it does not — roughly two orders of magnitude cheaper than discovering the same mismatch after the full gate band. |
| KI-E410 | **A fixer created and tested files successfully but never committed them, so the entire gate panel evaluated a diff that didn't actually exist in the tracked tree — all gates approved, but the "fix" was never really there.** Fix: added a mandatory git-status self-check to the fixer brief at the end of every file-creating step — any new/modified file not appearing in the status output must be corrected before proceeding. |
| KI-E411 | **A pre-band acceptance-coverage probe could fail because the test-author wrote a test that already passes on the OLD code (verifying pre-existing behavior rather than the fix) — a test-authoring defect misdiagnosed as a probe failure mode.** Proposed fix (not yet implemented): a static heuristic comparing the test's own notes/file list against the acceptance clause's key action words before the fixer is even dispatched, flagging an apparent mismatch for re-authoring instead of running a full, doomed fixer pass. |
| KI-E412 | **A test-evidence transcript file accumulates across every attempt in an item's lifetime, including attempts from different environments/machines — the fold's baseline-capture logic processes every historical suite-run marker in the file, so a single marker referencing a path outside the current worktree throws and overrides an otherwise-legitimate result to FAILED.** Workaround applied by hand once (trimming the stale historical content and re-folding under a fresh result id). Engine fix still needed: the baseline-capture logic should only consider the LAST complete suite run in the transcript, or the transcript should be cleared on redispatch so historical runs can never contaminate a fresh one. |
| KI-E413 | **Several selftest assertions went stale after unrelated feature changes updated runtime behavior without updating the corresponding pinned assertions — a recurring maintenance-debt pattern whenever a config default changes, a conditional file becomes absent, or a string a test checks for gets a suffix appended.** Fix: corrected each stale assertion to match the new behavior. Recorded as a general lesson: any change to a config default, a conditional code path, or a string a selftest matches against must audit for stale pinned assertions in the same change. |
| KI-E414 | **The architect review ran only after a fixer had already fully implemented the plan — if the plan itself was architecturally unsound, the full cost of test-authoring and implementation was already paid before the review could reject it.** Fix: added a pre-implementation architect review that runs on the PLAN itself (text, not a diff) between the plan-quality probes and the test-author stage, using a cheaper route than the full post-implementation review since the input is much smaller. One bounded plan revision is allowed on rejection; a plan that still fails fails the item before any test-author or fixer work begins. |
| KI-E415 | **The orchestrator crashed silently via a broken-pipe error when its stdout was piped to a short-lived consumer, leaving claimed items stuck with no worker and the scheduling lease held for its full timeout.** Fix: added stdout/stderr error handlers at the top of the orchestrator entrypoint so a broken pipe no longer crashes the process; documented the safe dispatch pattern as always redirecting to a real logfile rather than piping to a short-lived reader. |
| KI-E416 | **Claimed items whose worktree path no longer exists (e.g. from a since-removed external runner) stayed claimed forever — they never produced a checkpoint, never timed out automatically, and permanently consumed a scheduling slot.** Fix: added a startup sweep that scans every claimed item, checks whether its worktree path exists on disk, and auto-resets any missing-worktree item back to the ready state; errors are logged and non-fatal. |
| KI-E417 | **A scheduled dispatch job silently failed on every run for an extended period. Root cause: a shell idiom (`VAR=value cd somedir && command`) only exports the variable for the builtin `cd`, not for the subsequent command in the same `&&` chain — so the real command ran without the needed PATH, and a startup doctor-check correctly aborted every run, but the scheduling layer had no visibility into this.** Fix: wrapped the scheduled invocation in an explicit subshell with the export applied to the whole chain. General lesson: a `VAR=value` prefix before a shell builtin does not propagate past that one builtin. |
| KI-E418 | **A test run command could target one class name while the fixer created a differently-named class — only caught at fold time, after the full gate band had already run.** Fix: added a post-fix class-sync probe between the fix and verify phases that compares the run command's targeted class against the classes the fixer actually created/modified, failing immediately and cheaply on a genuine mismatch rather than letting the expensive gate band run first. |
| KI-E419 | **A startup sweep that auto-resets claimed items with missing worktrees (above) silently failed on every run, because it called the reset command without the controller lease token while the orchestrator itself already held that lease — the single-writer guard refused the reset and the failure was logged as non-fatal and ignored.** Fix: passed the just-acquired lease token into the sweep's reset calls; also fixed a related gap where a claimed item with a null (not just missing-on-disk) worktree path was being silently skipped instead of reset. |
| KI-E420 | **A production run went an extended period with zero closures while continuing to burn a large fraction of the token budget, with no automatic circuit breaker in place.** Fix: added a zero-closure-streak tracker that counts consecutive batch runs closing nothing; after a configurable threshold of consecutive zero-closure runs, the orchestrator automatically writes a stop-request marker file, halting further dispatch until a human reviews and removes it. A batch that closes at least one item resets the streak. |
| KI-E421 | **A class-existence probe (catching a test-run-target vs actual-class mismatch) failed the item outright even when a cheap auto-correction could have recovered it without a full retry cycle.** Fix: when the probe finds the targeted class doesn't exist, it now greps the test-author's own declared test files for the actual class name; if exactly one candidate class is found and differs from the target, the run command is auto-corrected in place and the pipeline continues — ambiguous (zero or multiple candidates) cases still fail as before. |
| KI-E422 | **A sweep that resets claimed items with missing worktrees treated ALL such items identically, including ones originally dispatched from an external runner whose paths never exist on this host — resetting them to ready caused immediate, guaranteed-to-fail-again local redispatch and wasted a large amount of tokens.** Fix: items whose worktree path matches an external-runner path pattern are still reset to ready but tagged as such; the auto-fix retry logic now skips automatic redispatch for tagged items, requiring a deliberate human decision to clear the tag. |
| KI-E423 | **Items failing with the same error class on every retry were reset to ready and redispatched indefinitely by the auto-fix logic, which was designed for transient failures but had no circuit breaker for a structural, non-transient one.** Fix: when an item's error class is identical across all its retries, the auto-fix logic now blocks the item (requiring human attention) instead of resetting it to ready again, converting an infinite retry loop into a single human-reviewable state. |
| KI-E424 | **Items that had already been escalated for human review could be silently re-dispatched if an unrelated reset path (e.g. the external-runner-phantom sweep) moved them back to a failed state — burning tokens on items the owner had already reviewed and intentionally parked.** Fix: before auto-resetting any failed item, the auto-fix logic now checks whether the item's last recorded state was escalated and skips the reset (logging why) if so, so a previously-escalated item is never silently re-attempted. |
| KI-E425 | **Items with repeated adversarial-review HIGH findings were reset to ready and redispatched by the auto-fix logic instead of being escalated, keeping them in an expensive retry loop for an unresolved quality issue the auto-fixer could not actually address.** Fix: the auto-fix logic now detects repeated HIGH adversarial findings across attempts and escalates the item for human review after a small number of repeats, rather than continuing to retry. |
| KI-E426 | **Agents running under file-write isolation repeatedly delivered their work to the wrong worktree when an item already had its own dedicated worktree — a structural conflict between two competing worktree notices (the item's assigned one and the write-isolation sandbox's own) that extensive prompt hardening alone could not reliably prevent.** Fix: write isolation is now skipped entirely when the agent's assigned path is already a dedicated worktree (that worktree IS the isolation boundary) — the sandbox is only still used as a fallback when no dedicated worktree exists and writes would otherwise land on the shared main checkout. |
| KI-E427 | **The class-name auto-correction probe (above) failed when the test-author's own declared test-file list was empty, falling through to a hard failure that forced a full expensive retry instead of a cheap auto-correction.** Fix: when the declared test-file list is empty, the probe falls back to a whole-worktree grep for test-like files; if exactly one candidate class is found there, the auto-correction proceeds as before — otherwise the original hard-failure path still fires. |
| KI-E428 | **A post-fix class-sync probe correctly detected when a fixer renamed a test class, but only ever failed the item outright asking a human to fix the run command, instead of auto-correcting an unambiguous rename the same way a sibling probe already does.** Fix: when exactly one new class name is found, the run command is now auto-updated and persisted in place and the pipeline continues; an ambiguous (multiple new names) case still fails with a message explaining why it can't auto-correct. |
| KI-E429 | **A fix intended to skip redundant write-isolation sandboxing when an item has its own dedicated worktree never actually took effect, because the worktree path was read via a property-access pattern that assumes an object shape while the real stored value is a plain string — so the check silently always fell through to the old (broken) behavior.** Fix: the path-reading logic now explicitly handles both the plain-string and legacy-object storage shapes. |
| KI-E430 | **When a targeted post-fix test failed, the item failed immediately with no recovery path — even when the gap was small (a handful of file changes: a missing test-double claim, a missing config key, a missing validation rule) and could plausibly have been closed inline instead of costing a full expensive retry cycle.** Fix: added a bounded micro-fix attempt before the final failure verdict — when the targeted test fails, a fixer is dispatched with a small-gap-only prompt (diagnose, apply at most a few targeted edits, re-run only the targeted test); if that succeeds the item proceeds normally, otherwise the original failure path fires as before. A guard flag prevents this from looping. |
| KI-E431 | **Agents were implicitly discouraged from using infrastructure tools (container/orchestration commands, local scripts) because the only stated constraint ("never mutate git") was broadly over-interpreted as "don't touch external state at all," even when a fix genuinely required cluster- or container-level verification.** Fix: added an explicit "infrastructure manipulation is allowed" section to the relevant agent briefs, clarifying that the only preserved constraint is the no-mutating-git rule on worktrees — infra tooling itself is fair game when the fix needs it. |
| KI-E432 | **A large token-spend analysis found five structural failure patterns across a long run with very few closures: a vacuous-test-scan pattern that didn't recognize a common mocking-library assertion style as a real assertion; a meaningful share of budget going to items that end up escalated after multiple expensive full cycles; one item passing every gate then being killed at fold for a missing evidence marker; several adversarial-review rejections where the fixer had not self-reviewed against common defect classes before declaring the fix complete; and a large share of budget in runs where zero files were ultimately changed.** Fixes shipped in this pass: broadened the vacuous-test-scan pattern to recognize the missing assertion style; added an explicit adversarial self-review checklist (race conditions, boundary values, error paths, null inputs, tenant/scope leakage, cancellation propagation, event payload correctness, dead writes) with a mandatory self-report field to the fixer brief. The other patterns were assessed as already covered by existing mechanisms or deferred as higher-risk/lower-certainty fixes. |
| KI-E433 | **Items that completed every gate but failed at the final fold step (for a missing evidence marker) were immediately redispatched for an identical, predictable failure; separately, items already in an escalated state were allowed to consume another full expensive pipeline run after escalation, despite needing human judgment first.** Fix: added explicit error-class detection for this fold-time failure pattern so the auto-fix logic can recognize it; on this error class, the item is escalated immediately instead of blindly reset to ready UNLESS the missing evidence has since appeared on disk (in which case a normal reset is allowed); separately, a config flag now excludes escalated items from automatic re-inclusion in dispatch batches, requiring a deliberate operator decision to re-enable per targeted run. |
| KI-E434 | **An item's baseline-compile pre-flight check correctly detected pre-existing compile errors in files outside the item's own claimed scope, aborted cleanly each time, but nothing ever fixed the underlying broken files — so every item touching that area cycled indefinitely, each cycle burning one cheap probe call for no progress.** Fix: the baseline-compile pre-flight probe itself was already correct; the actual fix was finding and repairing the small number of genuinely broken files at the source (a few missing field initializers and duplicate/ambiguous constructors) so the shared build is clean again. General lesson: a correctly-aborting pre-flight check is not a substitute for fixing the root cause when the same blocking files keep recurring across many different items. |
| KI-E435 | **All gates in a review band ran concurrently even when an early gate's rejection made the remaining gates' output moot — items that failed any gate overwhelmingly failed at the FIRST gate, so the other concurrent gates' token spend was pure waste on a failing item.** Fix: added an opt-in sequential-gates mode where gates run one at a time, aborting immediately on the first rejection and marking the remaining gates as skipped (excluded from any infra-failure accounting) rather than running all of them to completion regardless. |
| KI-E436 | **The highest-severity items were dispatched autonomously exactly like any other item, despite being — by design — beyond autonomous fix scope requiring human architectural judgment; they consumed a disproportionate share of total token spend with zero closures.** Fix: added an opt-in lockout flag excluding the highest-severity items from automatic scheduler selection entirely; such an item is only reachable via an explicit, deliberately-named dispatch request. |
| KI-E437 | **A `--help` flag on the run command was not checked early enough — it was processed as a real dispatch, accidentally claiming and launching several real items before the mistake was noticed.** Fix: added an early help-flag check at the very top of the run command handler, before any ledger read or item claim occurs. |
| KI-E438 | **Failed items with an unaddressed root cause were repeatedly redispatched by the scheduler, guaranteeing an identical failure and burning full pipeline cost each time — the scheduler's exploration bias actively favored retrying known-bad items because they had a zero historical close rate.** Fix: added a staleness check that reads a failed item's last-failure record and checks whether any commit has touched its claimed files since that failure; if not (and the failure wasn't itself an infra issue), the item is skipped from scheduling until a real code change lands on its claimed files, with an append-only log recording each prevented redispatch for later analysis. |
| KI-E439 | **Agent briefs were narrowly scoped to "only your assigned item," with no obligation to surface real issues an agent happened to notice alongside its own work — letting quality problems accumulate invisibly outside the system's own self-maintenance loop.** Fix: extended the assignment-focus rule in the planner and fixer briefs to add a same-file sweep (fix adjacent issues in files already being edited) and a discovery-surfacing requirement (issues outside scope go into an explicit returned field, never silently dropped); added the same "surface additional findings" field to every gate agent's brief. |
| KI-E440 | **Three token-reduction optimizations eliminating pipeline waste: items where the fixer made zero code changes (the underlying code already satisfied the acceptance criterion) still ran the entire pipeline including adversarial-review passes that have no diff to review; mechanical items used the highest reasoning-effort tier for planning/test-authoring despite being trivially bounded; and mechanical items used the most expensive model tier for the refuter regardless of severity.** Fix (A): when a fixer makes no code change and the pre-existing code passes the pinning test, skip method-level review flows entirely and short-circuit the refuter/re-auditor adversarial passes with synthetic pass transitions — the developer gate still runs to independently confirm the pre-existing code satisfies the acceptance criterion. Fix (B): downgraded mechanical items' planner/test-author reasoning effort from the highest tier to a middle tier. Fix (C): mechanical items now always use a mid-tier refuter model regardless of severity. Estimated combined savings: 40-50% on no-change items, 20-30% on mechanical planning, 10-15% on mechanical refutation. |
| KI-E441 | **An auto-generated effectiveness report was added to the factory's standard report set, tracking fixType close rates, severity close rates, first/second/third-pass success rates, gate-rework rate, adjudication rate, a token-reduction fast-path activation counter, band distribution, retry-exhausted backlog, and per-cycle throughput trend — all previously invisible cross-cutting health signals.** |
| KI-E442 | **Items failed the developer gate on trivial, cheaply-detectable issues (an unused import, a misleadingly-named test method, an assertion with swapped argument order or one that trivially always passes) that burned the expensive gate band before the defect was caught.** Fix: added a cheap pre-gate probe on all code-change items checking for exactly these three patterns; on a hit, a bounded fixer amend corrects them before the gate band runs. |
| KI-E443 | **A post-amend evidence check for the highest-cost item band used a lenient comparison that silently passed when no explicit suite-pass signal was present at all (as opposed to an explicit failure), letting items without real suite evidence slip through to the gate band.** Fix: for the highest-cost band specifically, the check now requires an explicit pass signal rather than merely the absence of an explicit failure signal; a lower-cost band keeps the original lenient check since it only requires a build-level pass. |
| KI-E444 | **Fixers occasionally reported files as changed that never actually appeared in the tracked diff — an edit that was never actually persisted to the worktree — which then surfaced downstream as a confusing drift/mismatch warning instead of being caught at the source.** Fix: added an explicit instruction to the per-step fixer prompt to run a diff-name-only check immediately before reporting changed files, and to drop any path from the report that doesn't actually appear in that diff, regardless of how confident the agent feels about having made the edit. |
| KI-E445 | **Items failed a pre-band class-name-sync check because the fixer created a test class under a different name than the test-author's run command targeted.** Fix: the exact target class name is now injected as an explicit named constraint into the fixer's prompt, stating that the test class must be named exactly that (or the run command must be updated to match) before the sync probe fires. |
| KI-E446 | **Items occasionally failed an edge-case review a second time after a first bounded amend already addressed the first round of findings — because only one amend round existed, the second round's surviving findings propagated straight to the expensive gate band.** Fix: added a second, capped amend-and-rescan round that fires only if the first rescan still finds issues; the pipeline never loops a third time. |
| KI-E447 | **Items failed the architect gate because the fixer stripped an inline "divergence" marker comment from code it edited — these markers are required by the project's own documented standards-evolution process whenever the underlying tracked decision is still open, and removing one without resolving the tracked decision is itself the violation.** Fix: added a cheap pre-band probe that scans the diff for removed marker comments of this shape, checks the referenced tracked-decision status, and (if still open) triggers a bounded fixer amend that restores every stripped marker to its original position before the gate band runs. |
| KI-E448 | **Planning and fixing agents had no access to a pre-built structural knowledge graph of the codebase when one exists, relying entirely on manual search for orientation — slower, more expensive, and more likely to miss cross-service structural context.** Fix: added brief instructions for both the planner and fixer to check whether a knowledge-graph artifact exists alongside the main checkout and, if so, read its summary report and the relevant local article before starting — skipped for quick one-file edits or code not yet reflected in the graph. Noted caveat: a structural graph is good for orientation, not a substitute for reading source for exact implementation detail. |
| KI-E449 | **Items were approved with only partial coverage of their own declared blast radius — a plan listed several components needing changes, the fixer only touched a subset, and no gate cross-checked the plan's own blast-radius list against the actual diff, producing a false "done" verdict on a partially-implemented change.** Fix: added a planner step requiring the blast-radius list to be explicitly cross-checked against the acceptance text and populated completely (with an escalation trigger if the true blast radius exceeds what the item can reasonably touch); added guidance to the fixer that an unaddressed blast-radius entry is a leftover, same severity as a stray TODO; added an explicit blast-radius-coverage check to the product-acceptance gate that fails the item if any declared component has zero diff coverage. |
| KI-E450 | **A post-fix class-sync probe incorrectly failed items whenever the fixer added NEW supplementary test classes alongside the originally-targeted one — which is correct, complete behavior, not a defect — because the probe's instruction flagged ANY differing class name rather than specifically the targeted class going missing.** Fix: the probe now only flags a mismatch when the originally-targeted class is absent entirely; additional test classes alongside it are explicitly fine. |
| KI-E451 | **A planner agent occasionally returned null on items requiring unusually large amounts of source-reading and cross-service context, because its model's context window filled and repeated compaction thrashing exhausted the attempt with no useful output — treated identically to a deliberate escalation, with no second attempt on a larger-context model.** Fix: added a null-plan guard that retries once on a larger-context-window model before allowing the pipeline to treat the failure as a real escalation. |
| KI-E452 | **Items occasionally had an already-complete, fully-approved result sitting unfolded while the ledger still showed a failed/escalated/stuck state — nothing detected this automatically, requiring a human to notice and manually recover it every time.** Root cause: a fold-tracking identifier is marked consumed the moment ANY fold processes it (success or failure), so if an earlier, worse-outcome fold (from a since-fixed bug, or a crash before its own fold step completed) consumed that identifier first, the item's own later, fully-approved result could never be folded through the normal path. Fix: added a reconciliation sweep that detects this shape (a non-closed item with a complete, all-gates-approved result sitting on disk), then — rather than trusting that stale result directly — drives the same manual-recovery scaffold and actually RE-EXECUTES a fresh verification pass; only if that fresh run independently reproduces a clean result does it close the item, borrowing the previously-approved verdicts; any discrepancy aborts and reports the item for a human instead of forcing a close. |
| KI-E453 | **A checkpoint-validity check rejected an item's own valid checkpoint whenever the final integration step legitimately repointed its recorded branch to the real shared accumulation branch — causing the batch-progress watcher to appear stuck indefinitely even though the item had actually closed cleanly.** Fix: the branch-match check now only applies to non-closed results (which never run the integration step and so must still match exactly); a closed result's recorded branch is trusted as reported, since repointing it there is the integration step's own intended, correct behavior. |
| KI-E454 | **A self-improvement proposal ranking used raw lifetime occurrence counts with zero recency decay — an issue that was common a month ago and has barely recurred since ranks identically to one that is equally common today, so a long-resolved pattern can outrank a live one indefinitely.** Fix: added an exponential recency-decay weighting (a configurable half-life, with a neutral weight for any record with no timestamp — never silently zeroed) applied to the ranking score for every proposal class derived from the cumulative historical log, while keeping each proposal's displayed count as the honest raw integer — only the ranking changes, not the human-readable fact. |
| KI-E455 | **A manual-recovery fold path (used when an item needs hand-driven recovery rather than the normal automatic dispatch loop) never fed the self-improvement learning trackers (hill-climbing, bandit selection, simulated annealing, prompt evolution, config optimization, recalibration, the zero-closure-streak counter, and post-batch failure analysis) — these only ever updated from inside the normal automatic dispatch loop, so a real closure via manual recovery left every tracker's state unchanged.** Fix: exposed the eight tracker-update functions from the dispatch-loop module; added a small CLI subcommand that invokes all eight with the same minimal shape the normal dispatch loop already uses; the manual-recovery fold path now spawns that subcommand as an independent process after every successful fold (not an in-process import, since the dispatch-loop module's own CLI entry executes unconditionally at load time); the normal dispatch loop's own call site was updated to skip its own redundant update when delegating through the shared fold path, so trackers are never double-counted. |
